## Letter of Acacius and Paul

A letter written in the ninety-second year of the reign of Diocletian, in year twelve of Valentinian and Valens and year eight of Gratian, to Epiphanius of Palestine, a native of Eleutheropolis, [once] father of a monastery in the district of that same Eleutheropolis and now bishop of the city of Constantia in the province of Cyprus, from Acacius and Paul, presbyters and archimandrites, that is, fathers of monasteries in the parts of Chalcis and Beroea in Coele-Syria, [who asked him] to write against all the heresies; and not they alone, but many [others] as well urged him and, so to speak, compelled him to come to this. To our lord, most honoured in all things, the most reverent father Epiphanius the bishop, Acacius the presbyter and Paul the presbyter, archimandrites, greeting in the Lord.

**Acacius and Paul 1,1** The sight of your godliness, father, would indeed have sufficed, for it has filled with spiritual words [those] who have shared in seeing you face to face, and has implanted in them the great longing that has reached even us.

**Acacius and Paul 1,2** But since the report that runs ahead of a disciple of the Saviour, proclaiming the scent of the fragrance of his words and deeds, presses us hard to be filled with both his sayings and his mind, we ought indeed to have come in person and shared in the grace given to you by God, as to the apostles.

**Acacius and Paul 1,3** But since the journey was prevented by bodily sickness and hardship, we were not able to come ourselves, casting ourselves down at your feet, and to hear and learn the holy spiritual words that proceed from your mouth.

**Acacius and Paul 1,4** For we believe that, had we been worthy to go up and hear you, we would have been strengthened in the way of life that we have taken up — if we are worthy to reach its goal.

**Acacius and Paul 1,5** Since, then, sickness has overtaken us, we entreat your great godliness: do not begrudge giving us a share of the gifts truly bestowed on you by the Saviour.

**Acacius and Paul 1,6** For we confess — not we alone, but all who hear as well — that in this generation the Saviour has raised up for us, in you, an apostle anew and a herald, a second John, to preach what ought to be kept by those who set themselves this course.

**Acacius and Paul 1,7** So, since our common brother Marcellus was pressed by so great a report and drawn by longing for your godliness, and since he belongs to our fold, and since we have him as servant for so long a journey, though he is newly instructed, we have entrusted [to him] this great boldness of ours, sinners as we are, towards you, the disciple of the Saviour.

**Acacius and Paul 1,8a** What we ask is that you grant us, for our instruction, some of the things you have said to certain brothers — a thing that is no burden to you, the righteous man, but for us sinners, once we have shared in it, a cause to rejoice in the Lord.

**Acacius and Paul 1,8b** For when we are filled with your spiritual words, we are lightened of our iniquities.

**Acacius and Paul 1,9a** We have heard of names given by your honour to the heresies, and we ask your godliness to make plain to us expressly the heresy of each of these cults.

**Acacius and Paul 1,9b** For the gift is [not] equal in all.

**Acacius and Paul 1,10** And we ask you — you, the righteous man — to offer prayer to the Lord on behalf of everyone who yearns for you and awaits the gift that comes from you.

**Acacius and Paul 1,11** We are in fasting and prayer that our common brother, once received by your honour, may gladly bring back the gift bestowed by you, and that so the customary prayers may be sent up to the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit.

**Acacius and Paul 1,12a** All the brothers hope to be strengthened by the prayer that you make on their behalf.

**Acacius and Paul 1,12b** We beseech you not to be slow to share, since you possess the divine grace of the apostles.

**Acacius and Paul 1,13a** All the little ones who are in the folds send up prayer to the Higher One that they may receive a spiritual gift from your godliness.

**Acacius and Paul 1,13b** May you continue strong in the Lord and of good cheer in Christ, and in the Holy Spirit [...] the throne given to you and the divine gift, until the crown that awaits you.

## Proem I

The reply written by [the] same Epiphanius to the presbyters Acacius and Paul and those with them, answering what they had written to him about his writing against the heresies. To my lords, the most honoured brothers and fellow presbyters Acacius and Paul, Epiphanius sends greeting in the Lord.

**Proem I 1,1a** Ancient writers, sketching a proem — or, in other words, a preface — as a kind of superscription, used to show through its riddling figure a glimpse of the whole treatise that lay before them.

**Proem I 1,1b** Hence we too, using the same form, make our preface to you, beloved, summing up in brief the substance of your request against the heresies.

**Proem I 1,2** For since we are about to make known to you the names of the heresies and to uncover the lawless deeds practised among them, which are like venoms and poisons, and at the same time to apply antidotes along with them — remedies for those who have been bitten and preventives for those who are going to fall into this — we sketch this very proem for lovers of the good, explaining the work as a Panarion, that is, a medicine chest for those bitten by wild beasts, which is composed in three books and [contains] eighty heresies, and these are figures of wild beasts or serpents.

**Proem I 1,3** “But one after the eighty” is the foundation of the truth, at once the teaching and the saving work and Christ’s “holy bride”, the church, which exists from everlasting, but which, as the times ran their course, was disclosed through the coming of Christ in the flesh, in the midst of the heresies already mentioned

**Proem I 1,4** and has been placed by us for remembrance through the preaching of Christ, and which again, after all the wickednesses of these heresies, for the refreshment of those who have gone through the toil of the heresies in their reading, has been set forth clearly by us in a summary statement according to the teaching of the apostles.

**Proem I 2,1** We beg all of you who read with a love of the good both the proem and the heresies that follow, and the defence on behalf of the truth, and the teaching of the truth and of the faith of the holy catholic church, to pardon us, who are only human and who strive eagerly, with toil and divine zeal, to make a defence on behalf of godliness,

**Proem I 2,2** if [...] anything excessive, wishing, as far as our own power allows, to defend the all-holy and all-venerable name itself — God granting pardon to us who seek what is beyond our power, since our discourse is for the sake of truth and our treatise for the sake of godliness.

**Proem I 2,3** And further, we beg you [to pardon us] if anywhere you find [us] — though it is not our habit to mock anyone or to jeer at them — speaking under provocation, out of zeal against the heresies and to turn readers away from them, calling some people impostors or charlatans or wretches.

**Proem I 2,4** For the very necessity of the contest’s arguments produces such sweat in us, in order to turn readers away and to show that their practices, mysteries and teachings are utterly renounced by our way of thinking, so that by our words and by the sharpness of our rebuttal we may both display our own freedom and turn some away from those same things, even by means of words that seem rather harsh.

**Proem I 3,1** The contents of the whole treatise run through the three books already mentioned, the first, the second and the third, which three books we have divided into seven tomes, and in each tome a certain number of heresies and schisms is contained, and altogether they are eighty, whose names and occasions are these:

**Proem I 3,2** first, Barbarism; second, Scythianism; third, Hellenism; fourth, Judaism; fifth, Samaritanism.

**Proem I 3,3** From these come the following: before Christ’s sojourn, and after Barbarism and the Scythian superstition, these that sprang from Hellenism: sixth, the Pythagoreans or Peripatetics, a school divided off through Aristotle and his followers; seventh, the Platonists; eighth, the Stoics; ninth, the Epicureans.

**Proem I 3,4** Then the Samaritan heresy, which comes from Judaism, and four nations of it: tenth, the Gorothenes; eleventh, the Sebuaeans; twelfth, the Essenes; thirteenth, the Dositheans.

**Proem I 3,5** Then the same Judaism mentioned before, which took its character from Abraham, was broadened through the law given to Moses, and received the name of Judaism as its lot from Judah the son of Jacob, who is also Israel, through David, who reigned from his tribe.

**Proem I 3,6** And from Judaism itself these seven heresies: fourteenth, that of the Scribes; fifteenth, that of the Pharisees; sixteenth, that of the Sadducees; seventeenth, that of the Hemerobaptists; eighteenth, that of the Ossaeans; nineteenth, that of the Nasaraeans; twentieth, that of the Herodians.

**Proem I 4,1** Following these heresies, and later as the times succeeded one another, our Lord Jesus Christ’s saving dispensation was made manifest, that is, his coming in the flesh, and the teaching of the gospel and the preaching of the kingdom, which alone is the spring of salvation and the faith of the truth of the catholic and apostolic and orthodox church.

**Proem I 4,2** But from this church these heresies have broken off and split away, having the name of Christ alone, and not the faith:

**Proem I 4,3** first, the Simonians; second, the Menandrians; third, the Satornilians; fourth, the Basilideans; fifth, the Nicolaitans; sixth, the Gnostics, who are also Stratiotics, and the same are Phibionites, among some called Secundians, among others Socratites, among others Zacchaeans, and among some Coddians, and Borborites and Barbelites; seventh, the Carpocratians; eighth, the Cerinthians, who are also Merinthians; ninth, the Nazoraeans; tenth, the Ebionites; eleventh, the Valentinians; twelfth, the Secundians, with whom Epiphanes and Isidore are joined; thirteenth, the Ptolemaeans;

**Proem I 4,4** fourteenth, the Marcosians; fifteenth, the Colorbasians; sixteenth, the Heracleonites; seventeenth, the Ophites; eighteenth, the Cainites; nineteenth, the Sethians; twentieth, the Archontics; twenty-first, the Cerdonians; twenty-second, the Marcionites; twenty-third, the Lucianists; twenty-fourth, the Apelleans; twenty-fifth, the Severians; twenty-sixth, the Tatianists;

**Proem I 4,5** twenty-seventh, the Encratites; twenty-eighth, that of the Phrygians, also of the Montanists and Tascodrugites; but these Tascodrugites in turn were divided among themselves; twenty-ninth, the Pepuzians, who are also Priscillianists and Quintillianists, with whom the Artotyrites are joined; thirtieth, the Quartodecimans, who keep the Passover on one day of the year; thirty-first, the Alogi, who do not receive John’s gospel and Revelation; thirty-second, the Adamians; thirty-third, the Sampsaeans, who are also Elchasaites; thirty-fourth, the Theodotians; thirty-fifth, the Melchizedekians; thirty-sixth, the Bardesianists; thirty-seventh, the Noetians; thirty-eighth, the Valesians; thirty-ninth, the Cathari, who are also Novatians; fortieth, the Angelics; forty-first, the Apostolics, who are also Apotactics; forty-second, the Sabellians; forty-third, the Origenists, who are also the obscene; forty-fourth, the Origenists, who are also those of Adamantius;

**Proem I 4,6** forty-fifth, that of Paul the Samosatian; forty-sixth, the Manichaeans, who are also Acuanites; forty-seventh, the Hieracites; forty-eighth, the Melitians, who are a schism in Egypt; forty-ninth, the Arians, who are also Ariomanites;

**Proem I 4,7** fiftieth, the schism of the Audians; fifty-first, the Photinians; fifty-second, the Marcellians; fifty-third, the Semi-Arians; fifty-fourth, the Pneumatomachi, who are also Macedonians and Eleusius’ disciples, those who blaspheme God’s Holy Spirit; fifty-fifth, the Aerians; fifty-sixth, the Aetians, who are also Anomoeans, with whom Eunomius is joined, or rather the Lawless One;

**Proem I 4,8** fifty-seventh, the Dimoerites, who do not confess the perfect incarnation of Christ, who are also Apollinarians; fifty-eighth, those who also say that holy Mary, the ever-virgin, was united with Joseph after she had given birth to the Saviour, whom we have called Antidicomarians; fifty-ninth, those who also offer a cake in the name of the same Mary, who are called Collyridians; sixtieth, the Massalians, with whom are joined the Martyrians, who come from the Greeks, and the Euphemites and Satanians.

**Proem I 5,1a** Going back once more and dividing them by book, I shall show in this summary of mine how many of these eighty heresies are in the first book, and then in order in the second and on to the third; and further, for every one of the seven tomes arranged in the three books, how many heresies it contains.

**Proem I 5,1b** And it stands thus:

**Proem I 5,2a** in the first book, three tomes and forty-six heresies, together with their [mother and archetypal] names, that is to say, those of Barbarism and Scythianism and Hellenism and Judaism and Samaritanism.

**Proem I 5,2b** In the second book, two tomes and twenty-three heresies.

**Proem I 5,2c** In the third book, two tomes and eleven heresies.

**Proem I 5,3a** In the first tome of the first book, then, twenty heresies, which are these: Barbarism, Scythianism, Hellenism, Judaism.

**Proem I 5,3b** Divisions of the Greeks: Pythagoreans or Peripatetics, Platonists, Stoics, Epicureans.

**Proem I 5,3c** The Samaritan heresy, which comes from Judaism; the nations of the Samaritans, four, thus: Gorothenes, Sebuaeans, Essenes, Dositheans.

**Proem I 5,3d** Heresies of the Jews, seven, thus: Scribes, Pharisees, Sadducees, Hemerobaptists, Ossaeans, Nasaraeans, Herodians.

**Proem I 5,4** In the second tome of the first book, likewise, thirteen heresies, thus: Simonians, Menandrians, Satornilians, Basilideans, Nicolaitans, Gnostics, who are also Stratiotics and Phibionites, and the same are called by some Secundians, by others Socratites, by others again Zacchaeans, and Coddians and Borborites and Barbelites; Carpocratians; Cerinthians, known also as Merinthians; Nazoraeans; Ebionites; Valentinians; Secundians, to whom Epiphanes and Isidore are attached; Ptolemaeans.

**Proem I 5,5a** In the third tome of the same first book, thirteen heresies, thus: the Marcosians, the Colorbasians, the Heracleonites, the Ophites, the Cainites, the Sethians, the Archontics, the Cerdonians, the Marcionites, the Lucianists, the Apelleans, the Severians, the Tatianists.

**Proem I 5,5b** This is the summary of the first book, of the three tomes.

**Proem I 5,6a** Of the second book, two tomes.

**Proem I 5,6b** And in the first tome of the second book, which is the fourth in the sequence of numbering from the beginning, eighteen heresies, thus: Encratites; Phrygians, who are also Montanists and Tascodrugites, though the Tascodrugites were divided from those mentioned before; Pepuzians, [who are also Priscillianists] and Quintillianists, with whom the Artotyrites are joined; Quartodecimans, who keep the Passover on one day of the year; Alogi, who do not accept the Gospel of John and the Revelation; Adamians; Sampsaeans, who are also Elchasaites; Theodotians; Melchizedekians; Bardesianists; Noetians; Valesians; Cathari; Angelics; Apostolics, who are also Apotactics, with whom are joined those called Saccophori; Sabellians; Origenists, the obscene; Origenists, those of Adamantius.

**Proem I 5,7a** In the second tome of the same second book, which is the fifth by the numbering mentioned before, five heresies, thus: that of Paul the Samosatian; Manichaeans, who are also Acuanites; Hieracites; the schism of the Melitians in Egypt; Arians.

**Proem I 5,7b** This too is the summary of the second book, of the [two] tomes.

**Proem I 5,8a** Likewise in the third book there are two tomes.

**Proem I 5,8b** In the first tome of the third book, which is the sixth by the numbering we stated before, seven heresies, thus: Audians, who are in schism; Photinians; Marcellians; Semi-Arians; Pneumatomachi, blasphemers of God’s Holy Spirit; Aerians; that of Aetius the Anomoean, to whom Eunomius, also called the Lawless One, was joined.

**Proem I 5,9a** In the second tome of the same third book, which is the seventh tome according to the division of the numbering of the tomes that we stated, and which is the seventh tome and the end of the treatise, four heresies, thus: Dimoerites, who do not confess the perfect incarnation of Christ, who are also Apollinarians; and those who say that holy Mary, the ever-virgin, was united with Joseph after she had given birth to the Saviour, whom we have called Antidicomarians; and those who offer a cake in the name of the same Mary, who are called Collyridians; Massalians; and the defence of the right faith and of truth in a summary statement, which is the holy catholic and apostolic church.

**Proem I 5,9b** Such is the summary and heading of the whole treatise against eighty heresies and of the one defence on behalf of the truth of the only church, that is, the catholic and orthodox church — a treatise composed in the three books set out here and divided into seven tomes.

## Anacephalaeosis I

These are the contents of the first tome of the first book of the refutation of the heresies, [in which there are twenty heresies,] as follows:

**Anaceph. I 1,1** First, the mother and archetypal names of all the heresies, the five mothers out of which the rest have grown — and the first four are these: [1.] First, Barbarism, which exists by itself, lasting from the days of Adam for ten generations until Noah.

**Anaceph. I 1,2** Barbarism is its name because the men of that time had neither any leader nor a single agreement; instead each person kept to his own course and became a law to himself according to the preference of his own will.

**Anaceph. I 2,1** [2.] Second, Scythianism, from Noah’s days and thereafter up to the time when the tower and Babylon were built, and for a few years beyond the period of the tower, that is, down to Peleg and Reu;

**Anaceph. I 2,2** these, having inclined towards the region of Europe, were assigned to the part of Scythia and to its nations from the time of Terah and beyond, and from it the Thracians have arisen.

**Anaceph. I 3,1** [3.] Third, Hellenism, which began from the times of Serug through idolatry, and moved, as each person at that time followed some superstition, towards a more civic order and towards customs and ordinances.

**Anaceph. I 3,2** However, when idols had begun to be set up, the races of men made gods of those whom they had then followed [...], at first sketching them out in colours and portraying those who of old had been honoured among them, whether tyrants or sorcerers or people who seemed to have done in their lives something worthy of remembrance, through prowess or bodily strength [...];

**Anaceph. I 3,3** then, from the times of Terah the father of Abraham, they introduced the error of idolatry through statues as well, honouring their own forefathers with images and fashioning likenesses of those who had died before them, at first by the potter’s skill, then copying them by means of each several craft — the masons by dressing stone, the silver- and goldsmiths by fashioning them out of their own material, and in the same way the carpenters and the rest in turn;

**Anaceph. I 3,4** (it was the Egyptians, and with them the Babylonians and Phrygians and Phoenicians, who first brought in this worship — the making of statues and the mysteries — and from them the greater part passed over to the Hellenes, beginning in the age of Cecrops and thereafter),

**Anaceph. I 3,5** and afterwards, much later, they proclaimed as gods the circle of Cronus and Rhea, Zeus and Apollo, and the rest in turn.

**Anaceph. I 3,6** They have been called Hellenes after a certain man, Hellen, one of those who had settled in Hellas and who gave the country its name; but, as others say, after the olive that sprang up in Athens.

**Anaceph. I 3,7** The Ionians became their founders, as the precise account has it, from Javan [...], a man among those who built the tower when the languages of mankind were divided; and for this reason they have all been called Meropes as well, on account of their divided speech.

**Anaceph. I 3,8** Later, further down in time, Hellenism settled into heresies — I mean those of the Pythagoreans, Stoics, Platonists, Epicureans and the rest.

**Anaceph. I 3,9** But the stamp of godliness existed at the same time, together with the law according to nature, living apart from these nations and setting itself apart from the foundation of the world until now, standing between Barbarism, Scythianism and Hellenism, until it was joined to the godliness of Abraham.

**Anaceph. I 4,1** [4.] And afterwards Judaism, which from the times of Abraham took its character by way of circumcision, was broadened in Moses, the seventh from Abraham, through the law given by God through him, and received the full name of Judaism as its lot from Judah, the fourth son of Jacob who was called Israel, through David, the first to reign from the tribe of the same Judah.

**Anaceph. I 4,2a** For the apostle, rebuking these four heresies, said plainly, “For in Christ Jesus there is no barbarian, no Scythian, no Greek, no Jew, but a new creation.”

**Anaceph. I 4,2b** Divisions of the Greeks:

**Anaceph. I 5,1** [5.] Pythagoreans or Peripatetics: Pythagoras taught as doctrine the monad and providence, and the forbidding of sacrifice to the so-called gods, and not to partake of living creatures, and to abstain from wine.

**Anaceph. I 5,2a** He also drew a distinction, saying that the things from the moon upwards are immortal and those beneath it mortal; and he taught transmigrations of souls from bodies into bodies, even into animals and beasts, and also the practice of silence for a period of five years.

**Anaceph. I 5,2b** Finally he named himself a god.

**Anaceph. I 6,1** 6. The Platonists: God and matter and form, and that the world is generated and perishable, while the soul is ungenerated, immortal and divine; and that it has three parts, the rational, the spirited and the appetitive;

**Anaceph. I 6,2** that wives are to be shared by all, no man keeping one spouse as his own, but those men who wish consorting with the women who are willing; and likewise a transmigration of souls into bodies of different kinds, even into beasts; and at the same time they taught as doctrine many gods arising from the one.

**Anaceph. I 7,1** 7. The Stoics hold as doctrine that the universe is body and consider this perceptible world to be God, and some declared that he has his nature from the substance of fire.

**Anaceph. I 7,2a** They define God as mind and as the soul of the whole vault of heaven and earth that exists, and his body as the universe, as I have said, and his eyes as the luminaries.

**Anaceph. I 7,2b** And they hold that the flesh of all perishes and the soul of all passes from body to body.

**Anaceph. I 8,1** 8. The Epicureans supposed that atomic and partless bodies, homogeneous and infinite, are the beginning of all things, and they taught as doctrine that pleasure is the goal of blessedness, and that the affairs of the world are directed neither by God nor by providence.

**Anaceph. I 9,1** 9. Samaritanism, and the Samaritans who come from it; it derives from Judaism and arose before heresies were established among the Greeks and before their doctrines took shape, but after the religion of the Greeks existed, having taken its occasion in the midst of Judaism from the period of Nebuchadnezzar and of the Jews’ captivity;

**Anaceph. I 9,2** for they were settlers who came from the Assyrians into Judaea, and they received only the Pentateuch of Moses, which the king dispatched to them out of Babylon through a priest named Ezra,

**Anaceph. I 9,3a** and in all things they hold the same as the Jews, save that they abhor the gentiles and will not touch anyone, and save for their denial of the resurrection of the dead and of the remaining prophecies after Moses.

**Anaceph. I 9,3b** Four nations of the Samaritans:

**Anaceph. I 10** 10. Gorothenes, who keep the festivals at different seasons from the Sebuaeans.

**Anaceph. I 11** 11. Sebuaeans, at odds with the Gorothenes over that same matter of the festivals.

**Anaceph. I 12** 12. Essenes, who oppose neither of them, but keep the festivals indifferently with whichever they happen to be among.

**Anaceph. I 13,1** 13. Dositheans, whose manner of life follows the customs that the Samaritans also keep — circumcision and sabbath and their other observances — and who make use of the Pentateuch, but keep abstinence from living creatures more strictly than the others, and live in fasts almost without a break.

**Anaceph. I 13,2a** Some of them also keep virginity, and others practise continence.

**Anaceph. I 13,2b** They believe, moreover, in a resurrection of the dead, a thing foreign to Samaritans.

**Anaceph. I 13,2c** Seven heresies of the Jews:

**Anaceph. I 14** 14. Scribes, who were lawyers and repeaters of the traditions of their elders, keeping, in an excess of self-chosen worship, customs that the law had not taught them but that they had framed for themselves, as objects of reverence belonging to the ordinance of the legislation.

**Anaceph. I 15,1** 15. Pharisees, meaning separated ones, who lived the strictest life and were supposedly more approved than the others; among them there is the resurrection of the dead, as also among the Scribes, and assent concerning angels and the Holy Spirit, that they exist, and a distinctive manner of life, continence for a period, and virginity, fasting twice in the week, purifications of jugs and plates and cups, as also among the Scribes,

**Anaceph. I 15,2** tithes and first-fruits and continual prayer, and self-chosen forms of dress, through the cloak and the dalmatics or colobia, and the broadening of the phylacteries, that is, of the purple marks, and of the fringes and little pomegranates on the borders of the cloak, which were the signs of their continence for a season; and they also introduced the horoscope and fate.

**Anaceph. I 16** 16. Sadducees, meaning most righteous, who were of the stock of the Samaritans and also of a priest named Zadok; they refuse a resurrection of the dead and admit neither angel nor spirit, but in everything else they are Jews.

**Anaceph. I 17** 17. Hemerobaptists: these were Jews in every respect, but they said that no one obtains eternal life unless he is baptised every day.

**Anaceph. I 18a** 18. Ossenes, whose name is interpreted as most audacious.

**Anaceph. I 18b** These performed everything according to the law, but they also used other writings after the law, and they rejected most of the later prophets.

**Anaceph. I 19,1** 19. Nasaraeans, a name interpreted as “rebels”; they forbid all eating of flesh and take no part at all in living things; down to Moses and Joshua the son of Nun they use and believe the holy names of the patriarchs in the Pentateuch —

**Anaceph. I 19,2a** I mean Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, and those still earlier, and Moses himself, Aaron too and Joshua.

**Anaceph. I 19,2b** Yet they teach that the writings of the Pentateuch do not come from Moses, and they maintain that they have other writings besides these.

**Anaceph. I 20a** 20. Herodians, who were Jews in every respect, but who expected Herod to be the Christ and gave to him the honour and the name of the Christ.

**Anaceph. I 20b** This is the first tome, comprising what is set against all these twenty heresies; and in it also the subject of Christ’s sojourn and the confession of the truth.

## Proem II

**Proem II 1,1a** Of Epiphanius the bishop, against heresies: the work called the Panarion, that is, the Chest.

**Proem II 1,1b** As I begin to declare and tell of faith and unbelief, of sound belief and heterodoxy, I shall call to mind the beginning of the foundation of the world and what followed upon it, setting out not by my own power nor from my own reasonings, but as God, the merciful Master of all, revealed to his prophets, and through them has deemed us worthy of the knowledge of the universe, so far as human nature allows.

**Proem II 1,2** And as I begin at once to examine these matters, I find myself first of all in no small struggle; indeed I am greatly afraid, as I set my hand to no ordinary work, and I call upon the holy God himself and his only-begotten Child Jesus Christ and his Holy Spirit, that he may shine upon the mind of our lowliness for the illumination of the knowledge of these things.

**Proem II 1,3a** For the writers of the Greeks, poets and prose-writers alike, when they undertook some mythical composition, used to invoke some Muse.

**Proem II 1,3b** Not God, for their wisdom is demonic, and “earthly and not coming down from above”, as it is written.

**Proem II 1,4** But we call upon the holy Master of all things to take hold of our poverty and to breathe his Holy Spirit upon us, so that we may set down nothing counterfeit in the writing we have undertaken.

**Proem II 1,5** And having asked for this very thing, knowing our own inadequacy, we beseech the same to grant it to us “according to the measure of faith and according to proportion”.

**Proem II 2,1** Let the aim [...] for the reader regarding every question be this: that the findings are those of seasons and times, as much as our small mind, after long exercise, has been able to grasp, and that we by no means make promise of all the things in the world [...];

**Proem II 2,2a** for there are things unspeakable and things speakable.

**Proem II 2,2b** Untold they are, and not set in number, and, so far as it reaches men, beyond attaining, and known to the Master of all alone.

**Proem II 2,3** Our undertaking concerns the difference of opinions and of teachings, of faith in God and unbelief, of heresies and of the wrong-believing human opinion sown in the world by men gone astray, from the time man was formed on earth until our own time, that is, the eleventh regnal year of Valentinian and Valens and Gratian’s seventh.

**Proem II 2,4a** Of the things [about] heresies and schisms that are to come through us to the knowledge of readers, some we know from love of learning, some we have learned by hearsay, and some we have met with our own ears and eyes; and of some we are confident that we report the roots and teachings from an exact account, and of others some part of what goes on among them.

**Proem II 2,4b** Of these we have come to know one part through the treatises of ancient writers, another through the hearing of men who confirmed our understanding exactly.

**Proem II 2,5a** We have gathered this whole work of thought not undertaking it of our own accord, nor as men practised in promises that go beyond our scant powers of speech.

**Proem II 2,5b** For this very treatise too, which by God’s will we have been pleased to compose, [...] from lovers of the good, who in one place in one way and at another time in another have goaded our weakness and, so to speak, compelled us to come to this, as your honour asked when writing to us in letters of greeting, my most honoured brothers, most devoted to the good, Acacius and Paul, fellow presbyters.

**Proem II 2,6** Since, then, having examined from every side, not without God, the votes expressed in the requests, I have been deemed worthy to come to this because of the surpassing love of the servants of God, I shall begin to speak, not with eloquence of words nor with any glib utterances, but in simple speech and plain discourse, yet with exactness in the things set forth in the discourse.

**Proem II 3,1** Nicander the writer produced knowledge of the natures of wild beasts and serpents, and other writers [...] the materials of roots and herbs, such as Dioscorides the herbalist, Pamphilus, and Mithridates the king, Callisthenes and Philo, Iolaus the Bithynian and Heraclides of Tarentum, Crateuas the root-cutter, Andreas and Bassus Tulius, Niceratus and Petronius, Niger and Diodotus, and certain others.

**Proem II 3,2** In the same way we too, as we attempt to uncover the roots and opinions of the heresies, [...] not for the sake of harm to those who wish to read,

**Proem II 3,3** but just as the writers mentioned before made it their earnest concern not to point out evil, but to serve the caution and safety of human nature, so that people might know what is dangerous and deadly, and guard against it beforehand and escape it with the power of God, by taking heed and not engaging with such poisons when they meet them, or when they are assailed by such creatures through breath or bite or sight — those same men, taking thought for the same ends, [at the same time] also prescribed beforehand remedies of roots and herbs for the undoing of the malignity of the aforesaid serpents —

**Proem II 3,4** so too our labour, for the turning away of those mentioned before and of you, most beloved, [...] to uncover the forms of the dread serpents and wild beasts, their venoms and their deadly bites.

**Proem II 3,5** And in opposition to these we shall set out, briefly and as far as we can, here and there one or two sayings by way of antidotes, to beat back their venom and to save the one who wishes [...], after the Lord, and who by willing or unwilling choice has fallen into these same serpent-like teachings of the heresies.

## BOOK I (Sects 1-46)

### Sect 1: Barbarism

**1,1a** [1. Barbarism.]

**1,1b** For from the beginning Adam, formed from the earth on the sixth day and receiving the breath, was brought to life (for he did not, as some suppose, begin from the fifth day and was completed on the sixth; the thinking of those who say this is mistaken), and he was simple and innocent, possessing no other name, possessing no designation of opinion or of judgement or of distinction in way of life, [but] called only Adam, which is interpreted man.

**1,1c** From him a woman like him is formed for him,

**1,2a** from the same body, [through] the same breath.

**1,2b** Children, male and female, are born to him.

**1,2c** And having lived nine hundred and thirty years, he paid the debt that was due.

**1,3a** His child was Seth, and Seth’s son Enosh, and in succession Kenan, Mahalalel, Jared.

**1,3b** As the tradition that has come down to us [contains], from this point evil contrivance began to arise in the world: at first through the disobedience of Adam, then through the fratricide of Cain, and now, in the times of Jared and beyond, sorcery and magic, licentiousness, adultery and injustice.

**1,4** [But] there was no other opinion, no altered belief, but one tongue and one race sown upon the earth at that time.

**1,5a** To this Jared a child is born, Enoch by name, who “pleased God and was not found, for God had translated him”, and “did not see death”.

**1,5b** Enoch begets Methuselah, Methuselah Lamech, Lamech Noah.

**1,6** And the righteous judgement of God, bringing a flood of water upon the inhabited world, wiped out all flesh of men and of the other [...], but by its command it kept Noah safe in an ark, as one who had been well-pleasing in his sight and had found favour — Noah himself, already mentioned, and his three sons, Shem, Ham and Japheth, and his own wife and the three wives of the three sons,

**1,7** so that eight human souls were kept safe in the ark of that time from the water of the flood, and of every kind of wild beasts and animals, of cattle and of the other creatures on the earth, for the renewed establishment of the existence of all things in the world, of some two by two and of others seven by seven.

**1,8a** And so the tenth generation passed, a span of two thousand two hundred and sixty-two years.

**1,8b** And the flood ceased, and Noah and his household became a remnant for the world.

**1,9a** But there was not yet any heterodoxy, no nation at variance, no name of a heresy, nor even idolatry.

**1,9b** But since each man followed his own judgement (for there was not even one law: each became a law to himself and followed his own judgement, as the usage in the apostle has it, not of Barbarism only but of the others too; for he says, “In Christ Jesus is neither barbarian nor Scythian, neither Greek nor Jew”), the name given then, in the ten generations of that time, was Barbarism.

### Sect 2: Scythianism

**2,1a** [2. Scythianism.]

**2,1b** After the flood, when the ark of Noah had settled on the mountains of Ararat, between the Armenians and the Cardyaeans, on the mountain called Lubar, there the first settlement of men after the flood takes place; and there Noah the prophet plants a vineyard and becomes the founder of the place.

**2,2a** Children and children’s children are born to his sons (for it is not shown that he himself begot any more) up to the fifth generation, a span of six hundred and fifty-nine years, apart from Shem.

**2,2b** I shall go through the genealogy following the line of the one son: Shem, then, begets Arphaxad, Arphaxad Cainan, Cainan Shelah, Shelah Eber, a devout and god-fearing man, Eber Peleg.

**2,3a** And there was nothing upon the earth — no heresy, no opinion differing from opinion — but they were called men only, “of one lip and one tongue”.

**2,3b** There was only impiety and piety: the law according to nature, and the error that springs, according to nature, from each one’s own will, not from teaching nor from writings; not Judaism, not Hellenism, not any other heresy, but, so to speak, the faith that now lives as a citizen in the holy catholic church of God of the present day, which existed from the beginning and was later revealed again.

**2,4a** For to anyone who wishes, as a lover of truth, [...] to see [...], the catholic and holy church is the beginning of all things, from the very aim itself.

**2,4b** For Adam, [the] first-formed, was formed not circumcised but uncircumcised in the flesh; yet he was no idolater, and he knew God the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, for he was a prophet.

**2,5a** Not having circumcision, then, he was not a Jew; and not worshipping carved images or anything else, he was not an idolater; for Adam [was] a prophet and was aware that the Father said to the Son, “Let us make man”.

**2,5b** What, then, was he, having neither circumcision nor worshipping idols, except that he was shown to be displaying in outline the character of Christianity?

**2,6** So too must one understand concerning Abel and Seth and Enosh and Enoch and Methuselah and Noah and Eber, up to Abraham.

**2,7** At that time piety and impiety were at work, faith and unbelief: faith bearing the image of Christianity, and unbelief bearing the character of impiety and lawlessness, contrary to the law according to nature, until the time already indicated.

**2,8** In the fifth generation after the flood, then, when men were just multiplying from the three sons of Noah, in succession children’s children and their children became seventy-two in number, chiefs and heads in the world.

**2,9** Stretching out and pressing forward from the mountain Lubar and from Armenia’s borders — the land of Ararat, that is — they come to the plain of Shinar, where they chose [...]; this Shinar now lies in the land of Persia, but in former times it belonged to the Assyrians.

**2,10** There, then, joining together, they take counsel with one another to build a tower and a city; and since they had inclined from the region towards Europe into Asia, they were all named Scythians, after the designation of that time.

**2,11a** They set about the tower-building and build Babylon.

**2,11b** And God was not pleased with the work of their folly; for he scattered their tongues and divided them from one into seventy-two, according to the number of the men found at that time, for which reason they have been called Meropes, on account of their divided speech; and a blast of winds overthrew the tower.

**2,12a** So they were divided over all the earth, to right and to left, some going back to where they had come from.

**2,12b** Others had advanced to the farther parts of the east, and others again have taken possession of Libya;

**2,13a** so that, if anyone should wish to grasp the exact facts about these too, he would find how each of those who went forth obtained his lot in each particular homeland: how Mizraim succeeds to the lot of Egypt, Cush to Ethiopia, Put to the regions of Axum, Raamah and Sabteca and Thouan and Lud to the parts by the land of the Garamantes.

**2,13b** And so as not to extend the composition of the proem here to great breadth, I shall come back to the matter before us and take up the sequence again in order of succession.

### Sect 3: Hellenism

**3,1a** [3. Hellenism.]

**3,1b** Between Eber and Peleg, then, and the building of the tower and of the first city founded after the flood, at that very building there arises from then on the beginning of council, of assembly and of tyranny.

**3,1c** For Nimrod reigns, the son of Cush the Ethiopian,

**3,2a** from whom Asshur was born.

**3,2b** His kingdom was in Erech and in Arphal and Calneh; and he also founds Theiras and Thobel and Lobon in the land of the Assyrians.

**3,2c** The sons of the Greeks say that he is Zoroaster, who, advancing to the eastern regions, becomes the founder of Bactra.

**3,3** From this point the lawless deeds upon the earth have been spread abroad; for he became the inventor of evil teaching, of astrology and magic, as some say of this Zoroaster; however, as the precise account holds, this period belonged to Nimrod the giant, and the two stand far apart from each other in time, Nimrod and Zoroaster.

**3,4a** Peleg begets Reu, Reu Serug, whose name is interpreted provocation, from whom idolatry and Hellenism began among men, as the knowledge that has come down to us relates.

**3,4b** Not yet in carved images and in reliefs made of stone or wood or wrought silver or [of] gold or any other material, but only through colours and pictures did the mind of man devise evil for itself, and through free will and reasoning and intellect it devised lawlessness instead of goodness.

**3,5a** To Serug is born a child, Nahor, and Nahor begets Terah.

**3,5b** From this point the moulding of statues arose from clay-working and the potter’s skill, from the craft of this Terah.

**3,5c** Up to this point the age had come round to the twentieth generation, a span of three thousand three hundred and thirty-two years.

**3,6** And none of the earlier men ever died, a son before his father, but fathers, dying ahead of their children, would leave their sons behind as successors (and let no one speak of Abel, for he did not die a death of his own);

**3,7** but from the time that Terah set up a rival to God, fashioning it by his own clay-working, he received from justice the like of what he had done, being himself provoked to jealousy through his own child.

**3,8** For this reason the divine Scripture, marvelling, marked it, saying, “And Haran died before Terah his father in the land of his birth.”

**3,9a** Up to this time a certain Scythian succession and designation remained.

**3,9b** There was not yet any heresy, not yet any other contrivance, but only “[the first] fornication, the devising of idols”.

**3,9c** And from then on they deified either ill-starred tyrants or sorcerers who had beguiled the world with illusions, honouring their tombs,

**3,10** and long afterwards the circle of Cronus and Zeus, Rhea and Hera, and those around them; and then the Sauromatae of the Scythians, worshipping Acinaces, and Odrysus, the forefather of the Thracians, from whom the race of the Phrygians descends; whence too the Thracians are so called, after Tiras by name, who had a part in the tower-building.

**3,11** The whole time, with error taking its beginning from this point, came round to the season already indicated, [...] confounding the ways from then on; and writers and historians thenceforth, drawing their contributions from the heathen-myth error of the Egyptians [...], from which indeed the arts of sorcery and magic were invented.

**3,12a** These things were carried over to the Greeks from the age of Cecrops.

**3,12b** At this time there were Ninus and Semiramis among the Assyrians, contemporaries of Abraham, and the sixteenth dynasty of the Egyptians; and at that time the people of Sicyon alone were ruled by kings, and the beginning of their kingdom was Europs.

### Sect 4: Judaism

**4,1,1a** [4. Judaism.]

**4,1,1b** And God chooses Abraham, in the same character as the holy catholic church: faithful in uncircumcision and most perfect in piety, a prophet in knowledge, possessing in his life a gospel way of conduct.

**4,1,2** For at home he honoured his father, [but] when called by the Word he renounced even his own kin, obeying the one who called him, as did Peter and Andrew and James and John.

**4,1,3a** And so as not to lengthen my discourse again, I shall speak in brief.

**4,1,3b** For when he had reached ninety-nine years, this patriarch receives the divine command for circumcision, and from this point the character of Judaism begins, following on Hellenism; and this was, [from] the foundation of the world, the twenty-first generation, three thousand four hundred and thirty-one years.

**4,1,4** For Scythianism lasted from the flood down to the tower and Serug, and from Serug until Abraham and onward, Hellenism; but from Abraham there was not yet the name of a heresy, but only the name of his godliness: those who came from Abraham, then, were called Abrahamites.

**4,1,5** For he had eight children, but Isaac alone was the heir, because he both lived according to his father’s mind, devoted to godliness, and had been given to his father according to God’s promise.

**4,1,6a** Before him he had Ishmael by the maidservant Hagar, and Keturah bears him six children.

**4,1,6b** These were distributed over the land called Arabia Felix: Zimran and Jokshan and Ishbak and Shuah and Medan and Midian.

**4,1,7a** And “the son of the maidservant” (Ishmael, as I said, was his name) occupies [...] and builds the place called Paran in the wilderness.

**4,1,7b** To him twelve children are born, from whom come the tribes of the Hagarenes, who are also Ishmaelites, and are now called Saracens.

**4,1,8a** Isaac begets two children, Esau and Jacob.

**4,1,8b** And the race of the godly was then called Abrahamites and Isaacites.

**4,1,8c** When Esau had been set apart to Idumaea, the country lying to the south of Canaan and to the east, he becomes the founder of Mount Seir and builds a city, Edom, called Rokom and Petra.

**4,1,9a** Children are born to him, and they rule in Idumaea, each in succession, and they were called the chiefs of Edom.

**4,1,9b** From him, fifth in succession, came Job, Abraham being left out of this count and the counting beginning from Isaac;

**4,1,10a** for Isaac begets Esau, Esau Reuel, Reuel Zerah, Zerah Job, who was earlier called Jobab but later was called Job, a little before the trial that came upon him.

**4,1,10b** And circumcision was the rule of life.

**4,1,11** Jacob flees from the face of his brother Esau because of his wrath, on the advice of his father and mother, to Mesopotamia, to Padan, beyond Mesopotamia, to Souba; and from there he takes wives of his own kindred, four in number, and they bear him twelve children, who were also named patriarchs.

**4,1,12** As he was returning to the land of Canaan, to his own father Isaac and his mother Rebecca, a vision from God comes to him by the outflows of the Jordan (they call the torrent the Jabbok), where he beheld camps of angels.

**4,1,13** “And behold,” it says, “a man from evening, and he wrestled with him until the dawn” — and Scripture marked him as an angel; and by way of blessing he gives Jacob a name of dignity, Israel.

**4,1,14a** Rising up from there, Jacob named the place Form of God.

**4,1,14b** [And] since he who spoke to him through the blessing said, “Your name shall no longer be called Jacob, but you shall be called Israel” [...], and made it plain, saying, “You have prevailed with God, and with men you shall be mighty”, from this time they are called Israelites.

**4,2,1** And Israel, after Joseph’s going down into Egypt, himself also goes down with all his house, sons and descendants, and the wives already mentioned and others, seventy souls in number.

**4,2,2a** The race of Israel spends five generations in the land of the Egyptians.

**4,2,2b** For Jacob begets Levi and Judah and the remaining ten patriarchs.

**4,2,2c** Levi begets Kohath, Judah begets Perez; Kohath begets Amram, Amram begets Moses; Perez begets Hezron, Hezron begets Ram, Ram begets Amminadab, Amminadab Nahshon.

**4,2,3** In the time of Moses and Nahshon — the fifth generation, reckoned by Levi — Israel goes out of Egypt through the Red Sea with divine signs and encamps in the wilderness of Sinai.

**4,2,4** And when oracles had been given by God to his servant Moses to number the men from twenty years up to fifty who were able to draw the sword and to take up weapons of war, he found them to be six hundred and twenty-eight thousand five hundred.

**4,2,5a** At that time Inachus was known among the Greeks, whose daughter was Io, also called Atthis, after whom present-day Attica too is named; from her also comes Bosporus, after whom the city on the Euxine Sea called Bosporus is named; and the Egyptians call her Isis, and they worship her as a goddess.

**4,2,5b** There is also a river of the same name as he, called Inachus.

**4,2,6** From there the mysteries and initiations among the Greeks took their beginning, which had first been wickedly devised among the Egyptians and among Phrygians, Phoenicians and Babylonians, and were carried over to the Greeks from the land of the Egyptians by Cadmus and by Inachus himself, who was formerly called Apis and built Memphis,

**4,2,7** and which also took their beginning from Orpheus and certain others, and were later established into heresies in Epicurus and Zeno the Stoic and Pythagoras and Plato — heresies which, from the time they began, gained strength until the Macedonian times and the time of Xerxes king of the Persians, after the first capture of Jerusalem and Nebuchadnezzar’s captivity, and of Darius, and the time of Alexander the Macedonian.

**4,2,8a** For Plato was known at that time, and before him Pythagoras, and Epicurus afterwards.

**4,2,8b** From that point, as I said before, the writings among the Greeks took their occasion and came to be established, and after this time so did the much-vaunted heresies of the philosophers,

**4,2,9** which harmonize with one another in error and weave together a knowledge of one pattern, of idolatry and impiety and godlessness, yet by that same error are broken up among themselves, one against another.

### Sect 5: Stoics

Against the Stoics, the third [sect] from Hellenism, but the fifth in the series.

**5,1,1** The Stoics hold this view about the Godhead: they say that God is mind, or is to the whole visible expanse — heaven, that is, and earth and everything else — as the soul is in a body.

**5,1,2** These same men divide the one Godhead into many partial substances, into sun and moon and stars, into soul and air and the rest;

**5,1,3a** and transfusions of souls [...] and re-embodiments from body to body, as [souls] are purged out [of] bodies and then enter in again and are born anew, weaving this impiety into their doctrine through their great error.

**5,1,3b** And they consider the soul a part of God and immortal.

**5,1,4a** They had Zeno as the founder of the Stoa, about whom much is said.

**5,1,4b** For some said that he was [...] of a certain Cleanthes who came from Tyre, while others say that he was a Citian, an islander of Cyprus, that he lived for a time in Rome, and that later he presided over this doctrine at Athens in the Stoa, as it is called.

**5,1,4c** And some say that there were two Zenos, the Eleatic and the one mentioned before; yet both taught alike, even if there were two.

**5,1,5** This man too, then, says that matter is coeval with God, like the other heresies, and that there is fate and genesis, by which all things are governed and are acted upon.

**5,1,6** As much, then, as this brief composition of ours is able to set out as a protective remedy against his worthlessness [...], and I shall not rather drive the warrant of the treatise to great bulk; but I shall speak to him only in passing, so as not to stray into digression.

**5,2,1a** Where did you get the guidance for your teaching, sir?

**5,2,1b** Or what holy spirit from heaven has spoken to you about your error?

**5,2,1c** For you are straining when you claim two coeval things together, matter and God;

**5,2,2a** for your argument will fall and will not stand.

**5,2,2b** For you confess that there is a Creator, whom you also call Almighty, but you divide him into polytheism.

**5,2,2c** And of what could he be Creator, if matter were coeval?

**5,2,2d** For that which did not receive its beginning from any cause, and is not subject, will be its own master by itself.

**5,2,3** But if the Creator takes from it, having found it ready to hand, there would be a certain feebleness, and a borrowing out of want, in one who has supplied himself not from his own resources but from foreign materials lying at hand, so that he may bring his own handiwork into being.

**5,2,4a** And concerning the transfusion of souls, great is the worthlessness of your counterfeit thinking, you would-be philosopher who promise men knowledge.

**5,2,4b** For if it is a part of God and immortal, and you join wretched bodies — not only of [...], I might say, but also of wild beasts and serpents and base vermin — to its form, which according to you has its substance from God, then what could be more depraved than this?

**5,3,1a** You bring in fate, as though from it come the things that befall a man and others.

**5,3,1b** But by a single saying, put briefly, your myth-making will be overturned.

**5,3,1c** For if being wise, having understanding, being born rational or irrational, and all the rest come from fate, let laws cease; for fate rules adulterers and the others; and the stars that impose the necessity will pay the penalty rather than the one compelled to commit the deed.

**5,3,2a** But I shall speak of these matters in yet another way: let the schools stand idle, let sophists and rhetors and grammarians cease, physicians too and the other sciences and the untold multitude of mechanical crafts, and let no one teach any longer, if indeed the acquisition of the sciences comes to human nature from fate and not from the learning of letters.

**5,3,2b** For if fate has made the educated and most learned man, let no one learn from a teacher, but let the Fates who spin the threads receive the knowledge by nature, according to the utterance of your error, so boastful in words.

### Sect 6: Platonists

Platonists, the fourth sect from Hellenism, but the sixth in the series.

**6,1,1a** But let this have been said by me against Zeno and the Stoics.

**6,1,1b** Plato, who was likewise borne along the same lines, [...] the re-embodiment and transfusion of souls and polytheisms and the other idolatries and superstitions, did not perhaps think altogether as Zeno and the Stoics did about matter.

**6,1,2** For he acknowledges God, and that all things which have come to be have come to be from God who is; and that a first cause exists, and a second and a third; and that the first cause is God, while the second cause has come into being from God, [...] certain powers, and that by means of it and of the powers matter came to be.

**6,1,3a** For he speaks thus: “Heaven came into being together with time; so it will also be dissolved together with it” — overturning his own earlier statements about matter.

**6,1,3b** For he once said that matter too is coeval with God.

### Sect 7: Pythagoreans

Pythagoreans, the fifth sect from Hellenism, but the seventh in the series.

**7,1,1a** Before him, Pythagoras and those who profess the Peripatetic school describe God as one, but they devote themselves to other philosophies [...] and to the [...] philosophical teachings.

**7,1,1b** Again, he and those with him proclaim the same things as these, the tenets of a lawless and most impious opinion: immortalisations of souls, re-embodiments and destructions of bodies.

**7,1,2a** He finally ends his life in Media.

**7,1,2b** He says that God is a body, that is, heaven, and that his eyes and the rest, as in a man, are the sun and moon and the other stars and the elements in heaven.

### Sect 8: Epicureans

Epicureans, the sixth sect from Hellenism, but the eighth in the series.

**8,1,1** Epicurus, next after these, introduced the absence of providence to the world: that all things are composed of atoms and go back again into atoms, and that the universe exists and the world subsists by spontaneity, nature always generating and again being consumed and again arising from itself, never ceasing, springing up from itself and being crushed back into itself.

**8,1,2** And that in the beginning the whole was in the manner of an egg, while the spirit, coiled serpent-wise about the egg like a wreath or a girdle, then held nature bound tight.

**8,1,3** But at a certain moment it wished, by a kind of force, to bind the whole matter, or the nature of all things, more tightly, and so it split existing things into the two hemispheres, and from this the atoms were thereafter separated.

**8,1,4** For the light and finer parts of all nature floated up above, that is, light and ether and the finest part of spirit, while the heaviest and dregs-like parts sank downwards, that is, earth (which is the dry land) and the moist substance of the waters.

**8,1,5a** And that all things are moved of themselves and through themselves in the revolution of the pole and of the stars, as all things are still driven by the serpent-like spirit.

**8,1,5b** We have spoken partly about these things; and these four heresies are to be refuted in the same manner; [...] for the sake of brevity in the reading.

**8,2,1a** From then on, as I also said above, poets, prose-writers, historians, astronomers and those who introduced the other things of error, having prepared the mind for countless evil causes and practices, darkened and muddied the understanding of mankind.

**8,2,1b** And this became “the first error, the devising of idols”, and the wretchedness of teaching.

**8,2,2** All things were divided into Hellenism and Judaism; but Judaism was not yet so called earlier, but had acquired through Israel the name of the godly religion, until [five] successions had taken place.

**8,2,3** For Nahshon, who became chief of the tribe of Judah in the wilderness, begets Salmon, Salmon begets Boaz, Boaz begets Obed, Obed begets Jesse — the godly still being called Israelites — Jesse begets David the king, who was the first to reign from the tribe of Judah; from whom thereafter the kings in succession from his seed, son succeeding father, were established in order.

**8,2,4** Before David himself, the first in fact to reign in Israel was Saul, Kish’s son, from the tribe of Benjamin, [...] and no son succeeded him any longer, but his kingdom passed over to David, and through David, as the first, to the tribe of Judah.

**8,2,5a** For the first child born to the same Jacob is Reuben, the second Simeon, the third Levi, the fourth Judah, from whom they are called Jews, on account of the tribe of Judah, the race of the godly having passed over to this name.

**8,2,5b** So they were called Israelites and Jews.

**8,3,1** Up to this time the four generations of the earth were joined together, these four divisions having been distinguished in the former times up to the season indicated here by me, and beyond;

**8,3,2** that is, Barbarism from Adam to Noah; the Scythian superstition from Noah down to the tower and to Serug, two generations later than the tower; and afterwards, Hellenism from the tower and Serug and Aram until Abraham; from him onward, godliness joined to that same Abraham; and Judaism from his seed in succession, that is, from Judah;

**8,3,3** as the Spirit-bearing and holy apostle of God, Paul, bears me witness, saying in some such words, “In Christ Jesus there is no barbarian, no Scythian, no Greek, no Jew, but a new creation”, since from the beginning, when the creation was created, being new it had no name of any distinction.

**8,3,4a** And again, in agreement with these, he says thus in another place, “I am a debtor to Greeks as well as barbarians, to the wise as well as the foolish”, to show the Jews as wise and the Scythians as foolish.

**8,3,4b** And he says, “I am a debtor” [...].

**8,3,5** From that point the whole race of Israel bore the name of Jews, beginning in David’s time, and the whole race of Israel went on being called by the names both of Israelites and of Jews from the times of David and of Solomon his son and of Solomon’s son — Rehoboam, I mean, who reigned in Jerusalem after Solomon.

**8,3,6a** So that I may not be found merely passing by, leaving aside what concerns the religion of the Jews and not indicating the basis of their belief, I shall set down a few things out of many.

**8,3,6b** For the affairs of the Jews are, so to speak, most evident to all.

**8,3,6c** Therefore I shall not take on great labour to narrate this subject in the finest detail; but all the same it was necessary to set down here a few things out of many.

**8,4,1** The Jews, then, who are descended from Abraham in succession and have inherited his godliness, have the circumcision of Abraham, which he received at God’s command when he was in his ninety-ninth year, for the reason I stated above: that his seed, while sojourning in a foreign land, might not turn away from the name of God, but might bear a seal upon the body for the sake of remembrance and of reproof, so as to abide in the godliness of their father.

**8,4,2a** His son Isaac is circumcised at eight days old, as God’s decree prescribed.

**8,4,2b** And it is agreed that circumcision existed at that time by God’s command, but it was ordained then as a type, as we shall show about this later, proceeding in order.

**8,4,3** So the descendants in succession of Abraham himself continued to be circumcised and devoted to godliness — I mean, from him and in order, Isaac and Jacob and his children — both in the land of Canaan, Judaea and Philistia, as it was then called, but now called Palestine, and also in Egypt.

**8,4,4a** For Jacob, who is also Israel, goes down with his eleven children into Egypt, when he was passing his hundred and thirtieth year.

**8,4,4b** His other son Joseph had already been in Egypt beforehand, ruling as king, having been sold by his brothers through envy.

**8,4,4c** But the dispensation of God, working good things together for the righteous man, turned the plot against this same Joseph into a cause of wonder.

**8,4,5** So, as I said before, Jacob goes down into Egypt, and his sons and wives and descendants, numbered at seventy-five souls, as the first book of the Pentateuch of Moses relates, setting out all these things clearly.

**8,4,6a** They continued there for five generations, as I have often said; but now I have been obliged to repeat these things.

**8,4,6b** For the generations from Jacob remained, counted through Levi, from whom the priests were appointed, and through Judah, from whom after a time David the king ruled.

**8,4,7a** And Levi begets Kohath and the others, Kohath begets Amram, Amram begets Moses and Aaron the high priest.

**8,4,7b** This Moses, in God’s power, brings the sons of Israel out of the land of Egypt, as the legislation’s second book has it.

**8,5,1a** What the way of life of the sons of Israel was up to this time, however, it is not possible to say very clearly or with full evidence, except only that they lived in godliness and circumcision; even though it is written that “the sons of Israel were multiplied in the land of Egypt and grew common”.

**8,5,1b** For surely the time of their sojourning and intermingling, through slackness, produced that commonness.

**8,5,2** But what they should taste or what they should refuse, or all else that they were ordered to observe according to the distinctions of the law, had not yet been marked out with full evidence.

**8,5,3** When they had come out of the land of Egypt, in the second year after their departure they are counted worthy of God’s legislation through the hand of Moses himself.

**8,5,4** The legislation given them by God in the manner of a tutor (as indeed the law was a tutor, drawing its distinctions in bodily terms but holding a spiritual expectation) taught them to be circumcised, to keep the sabbath, to tithe all their produce and whatever was born among them, from man to beast, to give first-fruits, the fiftieth and the thirtieth, and to know God alone and to serve him.

**8,5,5a** The name, then, [was] proclaimed in a monarchy, but in the monarchy the Trinity was always announced and believed by the most eminent among them, that is, the prophets and the sanctified.

**8,5,5b** And they offered various sacrifices and services to God, the King of all, in the wilderness, in the worship of the holy tabernacle, which was constructed by Moses according to patterns shown to him by God.

**8,5,6** So the same Jews also received prophetic utterances about the Christ who was to come — called a prophet, but being God, and called an angel, but being the Son of God, and about to become man and to be numbered among his brothers — as all the divine Scriptures have it, especially the fifth book of the legislation, that of Deuteronomy, and [those] that follow.

**8,6,1** These Jews had, up to the return from the captivity of Babylon, these books and prophets, and these books of the prophets:

**8,6,2** first Genesis, second Exodus, third Leviticus, fourth Numbers, fifth Deuteronomy; sixth, the book of Joshua, son of Nun; seventh, that of the Judges; eighth, that of Ruth; ninth, that of Job; tenth, the Psalter; eleventh, the Proverbs of Solomon; twelfth, Ecclesiastes; thirteenth, the Song of Songs; fourteenth, First Kingdoms; fifteenth, Second Kingdoms; sixteenth, Third Kingdoms; seventeenth, Fourth Kingdoms; eighteenth, First Chronicles; nineteenth, Second Chronicles; twentieth, the Twelve Prophets; twenty-first, Isaiah the prophet; twenty-second, Jeremiah the prophet, with the Lamentations and the letters both his own and [those of] Baruch; twenty-third, Ezekiel the prophet; twenty-fourth, Daniel the prophet; twenty-fifth, First Ezra; twenty-sixth, Second Ezra; twenty-seventh, Esther.

**8,6,3** These are the twenty-seven books given by God to the Jews; but they are counted as twenty-two, like the letters of the Hebrew alphabet among them, since ten of the books are paired and so reckoned as five.

**8,6,4a** But we have spoken clearly about this elsewhere.

**8,6,4b** There are also two other books among them of disputed standing, the Wisdom of Sirach and that of Solomon, apart from certain other apocryphal books.

**8,6,5** All these sacred books taught Judaism and the observances of the law up to the advent of our Lord Jesus Christ.

**8,6,6** And they would have fared well, schooled as they were under the law, had they received the Christ who was proclaimed beforehand and prophesied to them by the tutor — I mean the law — so that, receiving his Godhead and his coming in the flesh, they might learn not the abolition of the law but the fulfilment of the law, since the law held the types, while the truth is in the gospel.

**8,6,7** For there was fleshly circumcision, doing its service for a time, up to the great circumcision — baptism, that is, which circumcises us from sins and has sealed us in the name of God.

**8,6,8** And there was a sabbath there, holding us fast for the great sabbath, that is, for Christ’s rest, that we might keep sabbath from sins in Christ.

**8,6,9** And there was a dumb sheep sacrificed there, guiding us to the great and heavenly sheep sacrificed on our behalf and “on behalf of the whole world”.

**8,6,10** And there was tithing there, kept securely, so that the iota might not escape us — the ten, the first letter of the name of Jesus.

**8,7,1a** Since, then, they were led by the type and did not attain to the perfection proclaimed through the law and the prophets and others and every book, they were cast out of the husbandry, and the gentiles came in, while they can no longer be saved unless they turn back to the grace of the gospel.

**8,7,1b** For all the ordinances have been abolished for them, as every testimony says, just as it stands in all Scripture.

**8,7,2** But we shall state briefly, from a single testimony, how irreversible and inviolable the sentence against them is, so that one may see exactly the verdict passed on them, as it says, “Whatever soul will not hear that prophet shall be utterly destroyed from its tribe and out of Israel and from under heaven”;

**8,7,3** for the Lord was to make fast, perfectly and for salvation, what had been handed down mystically through the law, and he who does not hear him and does not bear with him was to have no salvation, even if he keeps the law, since the law cannot make man perfect, because the ordinances in it were written in a bodily manner and are truly fulfilled in Christ.

**8,7,4a** Let this much be said by me about Judaism.

**8,7,4b** For I have mentioned a few things, so as not to pass over the whole of it in silence, but to point it out in part.

**8,7,4c** For their system and the refutation of it are plain, so to speak, to everyone.

**8,7,5** We have shown too the beginning, whence they took their origin: that from the first, from the piety of Abraham the patriarch, [the] God-fearing are called [Abrahamites], because they are of his seed, and from his descendant — I mean Jacob, who is also Israel — Israelites;

**8,7,6** and from the days of David, who reigned from the tribe of Judah, the twelve tribes, all of them, bore the names both of Jews and of Israelites, until Solomon the son of David and until Rehoboam, son of Solomon and descendant of David.

**8,7,7** And by God’s rebuke and on account of the unworthiness of Rehoboam the twelve tribes were split apart, and two and a half ended up with Judah — with Rehoboam, that is — and nine and a half with Jeroboam.

**8,7,8** The nine and a half bore the names of Israelites and of Israel, being under the kingship of Jeroboam son of Nebat in Samaria; whereas the two and a half in Jerusalem bore the name of Jews, being under the kingship of Rehoboam son of Solomon.

**8,7,9** And again there came successions of kings: Rehoboam begets Abijah, Abijah begets Asa, Asa begets Jehoshaphat, Jehoshaphat begets Joram, Joram begets Ahaziah, Ahaziah begets Joash, Joash begets Amaziah, Amaziah begets Azariah, who is called Uzziah, Azariah, who is also Uzziah, begets Jotham, Jotham begets Ahaz, Ahaz begets Hezekiah; and in the time of Hezekiah and Ahaz some tribes of Israel were led captive to the regions of the Medes.

**8,7,10a** After this Hezekiah begets Manasseh, Manasseh begets Amon, Amon begets Josiah, Josiah begets Jeconiah, who is also called Shallum, who was also named Amasias.

**8,7,10b** This Jeconiah begets the Jeconiah who is called Zedekiah and Jehoiakim.

**8,8,1** And let no one be in doubt about this, but rather let him marvel at the precision, set down here to the profit of earnest readers who, for love of learning, wish to grasp the exact sense of the Scriptures; these will be compelled at once, along with the benefit, to be grateful, having recovered the saying in the gospel which certain unlearned people, out of doubt, made to vanish as though by way of correction.

**8,8,2** For when the holy Matthew counted the generations threefold, saying from Abraham to David fourteen generations, and from David to the captivity fourteen generations, and from the captivity to Christ fourteen generations, the two upper numbers are found to be clear, containing no shortfall in the count; for they cover the times down to Jeconiah;

**8,8,3** but the third number is no longer found to have its full complement of fourteen generations in the succession of names, but thirteen, since certain people, finding Jeconiah together with another Jeconiah side by side, supposed that the number had been repeated twice over.

**8,8,4a** There was no repetition, however, but a clear count.

**8,8,4b** For the son had been called by his father’s name: Jeconiah son of Jeconiah.

**8,8,4c** So certain people, removing the one name as though for the sake of elegance, carried away by ignorance, made deficient what the account promises in the group of the number of fourteen names, and obliterated the rich skill of the arrangement.

**8,8,5a** From here, then, from the time of Jeconiah, comes the captivity to Babylon.

**8,8,5b** During this period of the captivity the elders came to Nebuchadnezzar at Babylon, begging that some of his own people be sent as settlers into the land of Israel, so that the country might not run wild with growth and become a desert.

**8,8,6** And he accepted their request (for he did not put it off) and sends four of his own races, those called Cuthaeans and Cudaeans, Sepharuraeans and Anagogavaeans, who went up together with their idols and at that time inhabited Samaria, having chosen this land for themselves because it was rich and most fruitful.

**8,8,7** But in time these, being torn to pieces by the wild beasts — lions and leopards and bears and the other vicious beasts — send to Babylon, begging, and marvelling exceedingly at the manner of life of [the] settlers who had dwelt there before, how they had been able to withstand the ravaging and violence of the beasts.

**8,8,8** And the king, summoning the elders, asked about the manner of life they had kept [during] their residence in Judaea, and how they had escaped the ravaging of the beasts, when such great attacks of wild beasts and such destruction were found in that land.

**8,8,9** And they showed him the legislation of God and with understanding laid open to him, point by point, their well-reasoned judgement, telling him that a nation could not dwell there unless it carried out the law of the God of heaven that had been given by Moses; for God was the land’s defender and did not wish transgressions to occur in it at the hands of foreign nations — idolatries and the rest.

**8,8,10a** And he, taking the matter in hand and being persuaded by the most true explanation of those who had instructed him, asked for a copy of the law.

**8,8,10b** And they gave it ungrudgingly, and along with the law send a certain priest, Ezra, from Babylonia as an instructor in the law, to instruct the Assyrians settled in Samaria (the Cuthaeans already mentioned and [the] others) in the law of Moses.

**8,8,11a** This takes place in the thirtieth year, more or less, of the captivity of Israel and Jerusalem.

**8,8,11b** So Ezra and those after him instructed the people in Samaria, and those who received the law through Ezra, who had come from Babylon, were called Samaritans.

**8,8,11c** And a span of another forty years passed, and the captivity was released, and Israel went up from Babylon.

**8,9,1a** And one is led to marvel how it came about that, corresponding to the four nations, four sects also arose in that very nation — I mean, first, of the Essenes, second, of the Gorothenes, third, of the Sebuaeans, fourth, of the Dositheans.

**8,9,1b** From here begins for me the treatise I have undertaken against the sects, and the cause how [...] I shall make clear in brief.

**8,9,2** What else is it but that, [just as] from the manifold diversity of speech of the tongues tribes arose, and in each tribe and line of fathers different nations were set up, and every nation set up for itself a king as its head, and from this it came about that there was an outbreak of wars and clashes of nations crashing against nations, each one forcing its own will to be preferred and the possessions of its neighbours to be carried off to itself, through the insatiable greed of this life that is in all of us —

**8,9,3** so also, at this time already mentioned, when the one religion of Israel and the Scripture according to the law passed over to other races [...] (I mean to the Assyrians, from whom come the Samaritans, the settlers), it came about that opinion too was divided.

**8,9,4** And thereafter error and dissension set out to sow, from the one piety, many counterfeit opinions, as it seemed good to each and as each thought fit to exercise himself on the letter and to expound it according to his own will.

### Sect 9: Samaritans

Against the Samaritans, the seventh sect from Hellenism, but the ninth in the series.

**9,1,1** The Samaritans, then, are the beginning of the sects that take their start from divine Scripture, coming after the Greek sects already mentioned, which arose without divine Scripture from men’s own reasonings, stupefied out of their understanding.

**9,1,2a** The whole race, then, were called Samaritans.

**9,1,2b** And Samaritans is interpreted “watchmen”, because they were stationed in the land in the rank of watchmen, or from their being watchmen of the ordinance of the law of Moses.

**9,1,3** And the mountain where they settled was called Somoron, and at the same time also Somer, after a certain ancient Somoron, son of Somer, for so the man was called.

**9,1,4** Now this Somoron was the son of one man of the race of the Perizzites and Girgashites, who at that time inhabited the land, being by succession children of Canaan, who seized this land now called Judaea or Samaria, which belonged to Shem’s sons, not being their own, because Canaan himself was the son of Ham, the paternal uncle of Shem.

**9,1,5** And it came about that they were called by the name Samaritans for different reasons: from Somer, from Somoron, from guarding the land, and from guarding the teachings of the law.

**9,2,1** These differ from the Jews first in this, that the Scripture of the prophets after Moses was not given to them, but only the Pentateuch given through Moses to the seed of Israel at the exodus, on the journey out of Egypt — I mean Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, Deuteronomy; and in the Hebrew tongue they are called thus: Bresith, Ellesimoth, Ouikra, Ouidaber, Elleaddebarin.

**9,2,2a** And in these five books the sign of the resurrection of the dead is sown, though it is not proclaimed clearly, and things concerning the only-begotten Son of God and concerning the Holy Spirit and against idols are sown in them; but [the teaching] concerning the monarchy has its introduction in them more clearly.

**9,2,2b** But within the monarchy it is the Trinity that is spiritually proclaimed.

**9,2,3a** And those who received the law were eager to be turned from idolatry and to acknowledge the one God, but they had no concern to know it more exactly.
