**30,6,6** Everything, then, was done through these two, because the patriarch, being a boy, was still a minor and was being brought up in their hands.

**30,6,7a** At this time, as Josephus’ mind was often troubled by the mystic rites performed in the matter of the washing, he considered what he should do.

**30,6,7b** And there was there a treasury building, sealed; and gaza is interpreted in the Hebrew language as treasure.

**30,6,8** And as many people had many ideas about this treasury because of the seal, Josephus ventured to open it secretly, and found no money, but books more precious than money.

**30,6,9** And reading in these, as I have already said, he found the Gospel according to John translated from Greek into the Hebrew tongue, and the Acts of the Apostles; and moreover the Gospel according to Matthew, which is Hebrew by origin; and having read from these he was again worn down in mind, being in some way distressed about the faith of Christ; and though he was already being pricked on two grounds, the reading of the books and the initiation of the patriarch, he nevertheless hardened his heart, as happens.

**30,7,1** While he was being worn away in these matters, the boy left by Ellel, being brought up for the patriarchate, grows up; for no one else seizes the offices among the Jews, but son succeeds father.

**30,7,2** So when the young man was coming to the prime of his age, some companions of his own age, idle and practised in evil, attached themselves to him to his ruin; perhaps, I think, he was called Judas, but I do not know very clearly because of the lapse of time.

**30,7,3** His young companions, then, led him into many evil practices, the seduction of women and unholy lusts, attempting to assist him with certain magical sorceries in matters of licentiousness [...], performing certain love-charms, and through incantations forcing free women to be brought under compulsion to his corruption.

**30,7,4a** Josephus and the old man with him, being obliged to accompany the boy, took it hard, and often brought reproaches against him in words and admonished him.

**30,7,4b** But he listened rather to the young men, hiding his shameful deeds and denying them; for Josephus and his companion did not dare to speak out openly about his doings, but admonished him as though by way of instruction.

**30,7,5a** They come, then, to Gadara, to the hot springs.

**30,7,5b** A festival is held there every year; for those who wish to bathe for some days come from everywhere, supposedly to be rid of their ailments, which is a stratagem of the devil.

**30,7,5c** For where the wonders of God have taken place, the adversary has been beforehand in setting his destructive nets.

**30,7,5d** For men and women bathe there together.

**30,7,6a** By chance a certain free woman happened to be in the bath, outstanding in the beauty of her form.

**30,7,6b** And the young man, bewitched by the habit of his licentiousness, passing through the steam, rubbed his side against the woman’s side.

**30,7,7a** But she sealed herself in the name of Christ, being of course a Christian — she who had no need either to break the law and bathe together with men.

**30,7,7b** Such things happen to simple and unlearned people through the negligence of their teachers, who do not safeguard them by their teaching.

**30,7,8a** Yet, so that God might show his wonders, the youth failed in his attempt — I mean the patriarch.

**30,7,8b** For he sent messengers to the woman and offered gifts; but she treated those who were sent with insolence and was not swayed by the vain efforts of the licentious man.

**30,8,1** Thereupon his accomplices, learning of the boy’s distress, which he had brought upon himself over the woman, undertake to contrive some greater sorcery for him, as Josephus himself related to me in detail.

**30,8,2** For they take the wretched boy after sunset to the nearer tombs; these are caves hewn out in the rocks, called in that country communal tombs.

**30,8,3** There the sorcerers with him, taking the aforementioned along, performed certain incantations and conjurations and practices full of impiety upon him and in the name of the aforementioned woman.

**30,8,4a** But by the will of God these things came to the notice of the other old man who was with Josephus; and he, perceiving what was going on, tells Josephus of it.

**30,8,4b** And first, bewailing himself, he said, “Wretched are we,” he says, “brother, and vessels of perdition: whom are we serving?”

**30,8,5** Josephus asked the reason, but did not hear it from him in words before the old man took him by the hand and led Josephus away to where the pernicious men were holding their gathering with the young man in the tombs for the sake of sorcery.

**30,8,6a** And taking their stand outside the door, they overheard what was being done by them, and withdrew when those men came out.

**30,8,6b** For it was not yet late evening, but about the very setting of the sun, when the eye could still see dimly.

**30,8,7** So after the departure of those most impious men from the tomb, Josephus and his companion went in and find certain devices and other devices of sorcery thrown on the ground; and having emptied their urine on them and heaped dust over them, as he said, they came out.

**30,8,8** And they knew the plot, and for what woman these evil things had been wrought by them, and they watched to see whether they would prevail.

**30,8,9** So when the sorcerers did not prevail (for the woman was helped by the seal of Christ and by faith), they learned that the young man had waited three nights for the woman’s arrival, and afterwards had quarrelled with those who had performed the sorcery, because he had not succeeded.

**30,8,10a** This became for Josephus a third work of instruction: that where the name of Christ and the seal of the cross are, the power of sorcery had no strength.

**30,8,10b** Yet he was not even then persuaded to become a Christian.

**30,9,1a** Then the Lord appears to him in his sleep, saying, “I am Jesus, whom your fathers crucified; but believe in me.”

**30,9,1b** But even so he was not persuaded, and he fell into a severe illness and was given up for lost.

**30,9,1c** And the Lord again appears to him, telling him to believe, and that he would be healed; he made the promise and recovered, yet once more persisted in his stiff-neckedness.

**30,9,2a** And he falls again into a second illness, and was likewise given up.

**30,9,2b** And being thought by his Jewish kinsmen to be dying, he heard from them what is always done among them in secret.

**30,9,3a** For an old man, one of those learned in the law, came to his ear and announced to him, saying, “Believe [in] Jesus, who was crucified under Pontius Pilate the governor, who pre-existed as Son of God and was afterwards born of Mary, and is the Christ of God, and rose from the dead; and that he is coming to judge the living and the dead.”

**30,9,3b** These things this same Josephus clearly reported to me in his account, as one may say with all love of truth.

**30,9,4** And I heard the same thing from someone else too, who was still a Jew out of fear of the Jews, but often spent time among Christians and honoured and loved Christians; he was my fellow traveller in the desert of Bethel and Ephraim as I was going up to the hill country from Jericho, and when I debated with him about the coming of Christ, he did not contradict.

**30,9,5** And when I marvelled and asked the reason (for he was learned in the law and able to argue), why he did not contradict but was persuaded about Christ Jesus our Lord, he too, on hearing so much, disclosed to me that when he was about to die he had heard from them, in a whisper into his ear, “Jesus Christ, the crucified, the Son of God, is going to judge you.”

**30,9,6** But let these things about them, in this discourse, stand set down by me here from a true report.

**30,10,1a** Josephus was still ill, and though he had heard from the old man, as I said, along with the rest, that Jesus Christ would judge him, he continued hardened.

**30,10,1b** But the Lord again, in his love for mankind, through a vision in a dream says to him, “Behold, I heal you; but arise and believe.”

**30,10,1c** But when he had risen again from the illness, he did not believe.

**30,10,2a** When he was well, the Lord appears to him again in a dream, reproaching him for not having believed.

**30,10,2b** And the Lord makes him a promise, saying: For the full assurance of faith, if you wish to work any divine sign by my name, invoke me, and I will perform it.

**30,10,3** There was a madman in the city, I mean in Tiberias, who went about the city naked, and often, when he was clothed, would tear his garment apart, as is the habit of such people.

**30,10,4a** Josephus, wishing to put the vision to the test but still hesitating, was held back by shame.

**30,10,4b** So he takes the man indoors, shuts the door, takes water in his hand, seals the water and sprinkles it on the madman, saying, “In the name of Jesus the Nazoraean, who was crucified, demon, come out of him, and let him be made well.”

**30,10,5** The man cried out loudly and fell to the ground, and after foaming greatly and being convulsed he remained motionless for a long time, and Josephus suspected that the man had died.

**30,10,6** After a time, having rubbed his brow, he stood up; and on rising and seeing his own nakedness, he tried to hide himself and laid his hands over his shame, no longer able to bear the sight of his own nakedness.

**30,10,7** And having been clothed in a garment from Josephus himself, from those he had, and having come to a settled state of understanding and sound mind, he gave many thanks to the man and to God, knowing that through him he had obtained his recovery, and spread the man’s fame in the city; and this sign became known to the Jews there.

**30,10,8a** And much talk followed in the city, people saying that Josephus, having opened the treasuries and found the name of God written there and read it, is working great signs.

**30,10,8b** What they said was true, but not in the way they supposed.

**30,10,9a** Yet Josephus still persisted in hardening his heart.

**30,10,9b** But God, the lover of mankind, who always contrives good occasions for those who love him, provides these for people who are counted worthy of life.

**30,11,1** For it befell Josephus himself that, after Judas the patriarch whom we mentioned before had grown up (for that, perhaps, was what he was called), he gave Josephus, by way of recompense, the revenues of the apostolate as a privilege.

**30,11,2** And he is sent with letters to the land of the Cilicians; and having gone up there, he collected from each city of Cilicia the tithes and the firstfruits from the Jews in the province.

**30,11,3a** At this time he lodges quite near the church — I cannot say in what city.

**30,11,3b** And he makes friends with the bishop there, and secretly asked him for the gospels and read them.

**30,11,4** Since, then, as an apostle (for so, as I said, the dignity is called among them), [being] most strict and, as it seemed, pure, he kept putting forward measures for establishing good order, deposing and removing from their rank many of the appointed rulers of synagogues and priests and elders and azanites (which among them is interpreted as ministers or attendants), he was resented by many, who, as though trying to retaliate against him, took no small pains to pry into his affairs and track down what he was doing.

**30,11,5** For this reason, having pried, they burst all at once into his house, into his own dwelling, and find him going through the gospels; and they seize the book and lay hold of the man, dragging him on the ground and shouting, and inflicting no ordinary injuries they lead him away to the synagogue and scourge him according to the law.

**30,11,6a** And this becomes his first contest; but the bishop of the city came upon them and rescued him.

**30,11,6b** On another occasion they seize him on a journey, as he related to us, and throw him into the river Cydnus.

**30,11,6c** And him, carried off by the current [...], they supposed to have perished, having gone under the waters; and they rejoiced at this.

**30,11,7** But a little later he is counted worthy of the holy washing (for he was saved), and he goes up to the court and becomes a friend of the emperor Constantine, and told him everything about himself: how he was of the highest rank among the Jews, and how visions of God had appeared to him in every way, as the Lord called him to the holy calling and to the salvation of faith in him and knowledge of him.

**30,11,8** And the good emperor, who was in truth a servant of Christ and possessed, among kings, a godly zeal after David and Hezekiah and Josiah, gives him, as I have already said, the privilege of a rank in his empire.

**30,11,9** For he appointed him count and bade him ask for whatever else he wanted; but he asked for nothing except to obtain from the emperor this very great favour: to be permitted by imperial decree to build churches of Christ in the cities and villages of the Jews, where nobody had ever managed to set up churches, because there was neither Greek nor Samaritan nor Christian among them.

**30,11,10** And this is observed especially in Tiberias and in Diocaesarea, also called Sepphoris, and in Nazareth and in Capernaum: [that] among them there should be no one of another nation.

**30,12,1** Josephus, having received the letters and the authority along with the rank, came to Tiberias, having also letters authorizing him to draw on the imperial funds, and being himself also honoured with allowances from the emperor.

**30,12,2a** So he begins to build in Tiberias.

**30,12,2b** A very large temple already existed in the city — perhaps, I think, they called it the Hadrianeum; and since this Hadrianeum had remained unfinished, the citizens were perhaps trying to convert it into a public bath.

**30,12,3** Finding this, Josephus took his opportunity from it; and since he found the walls already raised on four sides to a certain height with four-foot stones, he begins from there to see to the building of the church.

**30,12,4a** There was need of lime and of the other materials.

**30,12,4b** So he ordered many kilns to be made outside the city, perhaps seven in number (they call them furnakes locally).

**30,12,4c** But the Jews, clever and bold for any enterprise, did not refrain from the sorcery that is always practised among them.

**30,12,4d** For the noble Jews laboured by certain magic and sorceries to bind the fire, but did not wholly succeed.

**30,12,5** At any rate, the fire was idle and did not work, but became, so to speak, outside its own nature.

**30,12,6** And when those who had been ordered to put the combustible material on the fire (I mean brushwood, or kindling) reported to Josephus what had happened, the man, stung in his mind and roused by his zeal for the Lord, ran outside the city and ordered water to be brought in a vessel — I mean a flask (the local people call it a cacubium) — [and] taking the same vessel of water in front of everyone (a crowd of Jews had gathered for the spectacle, eager to see what would come of it and what Josephus would attempt to do), the man, with his own finger, made the seal of the cross over the vessel and, calling on the name of Jesus, said thus with a loud voice:

**30,12,7** “In the name of Jesus the Nazoraean, whom my fathers, and the fathers of all these who stand around, crucified, let power come to be in this water for the setting aside of all the sorcery and magic which these men have practised, and for the working of power in the fire, so that the house of the Lord may be brought to completion.”

**30,12,8a** And so he takes the water in his hand and sprinkles some of the water on each furnace.

**30,12,8b** And the spells were undone, and the fire blazed up before them all.

**30,12,8c** And the crowds that were present cried out, “One God, who helps the Christians!”, and went away.

**30,12,9a** But since those men often did the man harm, he at last built a certain part of the temple in Tiberias and completed a small church, and so withdrew from there, and coming to Scythopolis he settled there.

**30,12,9b** And in Diocaesarea and in certain other towns he built to completion.

**30,12,10** These things were done, and I have called them to mind here on account of the translation of the books — how the Gospel according to John passed from Greek into Hebrew, and the Acts of the Apostles — so let the matter of the history and the narration rest here.

**30,13,1** But I return to carrying on the sequence of my account against Ebion; for the discourse, as it went forward, compelled me, on account of the Gospel according to Matthew, to set down the sequence of the knowledge that has come to us.

**30,13,2a** In the gospel among them, at any rate, which is named ‘according to Matthew’ — not whole and entirely complete, but falsified and mutilated (and they call it Hebrew) — it is recorded that “There appeared a certain man, Jesus by name, himself some thirty years old, who selected us.

**30,13,2b** And coming into Capernaum he entered the house of Simon who was surnamed Peter, and opening his mouth he said:

**30,13,3a** ‘Going by the lake of Tiberias, I selected John and James, Zebedee’s sons, and Simon and Andrew and [...] Thaddaeus and Simon the Zealot and Judas Iscariot; and you, Matthew, as you sat at the tax office, I called, and you followed me.

**30,13,3b** I wish you, then, to be twelve apostles for a testimony to Israel’”;

**30,13,4a** and: “John appeared baptizing, and Pharisees went out to him and were baptized, and all Jerusalem.

**30,13,4b** And John had clothing made from camel’s hair, and a belt of leather round his loins.

**30,13,4c** And his food, it says, was wild honey, which tasted of manna, like a cake made with oil”;

**30,13,5** so that, no doubt, they may turn the word of truth into a lie and make it a cake in honey instead of locusts.

**30,13,6** And the beginning of the gospel among them runs: “It happened in the days when Herod was king over Judaea, [while Caiaphas was high priest,] that there came [a certain man,] John [by name,] baptizing with a baptism of repentance in the Jordan river; he was reputed to come of the family of Aaron the priest, a child of Zacharias and Elizabeth, and all were going out to him.”

**30,13,7a** And after saying many things it goes on: “When the people had been baptized, Jesus also came and was baptized by John.

**30,13,7b** And when he rose from the water, the heavens opened, and he beheld the Holy Spirit in a dove’s likeness, coming down and entering into him.

**30,13,7c** And a voice from heaven, saying: ‘You are my beloved Son, in you I am well pleased’; and again: ‘Today I have begotten you.’

**30,13,7d** And at once a great light shone round the place.

**30,13,7e** On seeing it, it says, John says to him: ‘Who are you, Lord?’

**30,13,7f** And again a voice from heaven to him: ‘This is my beloved Son, on whom I am well pleased.’

**30,13,8a** And then, it says, John, falling down before him, said: ‘I beg you, Lord, baptize me yourself.’

**30,13,8b** But he prevented him, saying: ‘Let it be, for thus it is fitting that all things be fulfilled.’”

**30,14,1** But see their teaching, falsified on every side, how it is all lame and crooked and has no straightness at all.

**30,14,2a** For Cerinthus and Carpocrates, who use, supposedly, the same gospel among them [...], wish to establish from the beginning of the Gospel according to Matthew, by means of the genealogy, that Christ is of the seed of Joseph and Mary.

**30,14,2b** But these people have some other notions.

**30,14,3** For they cut away the genealogies in Matthew and make their beginning, as we said before, with the words: “It happened,” it says, “in the days when Herod was king over Judaea, while Caiaphas was high priest, that there came a certain man, John by name, baptizing with a baptism of repentance in the Jordan river,” and what follows;

**30,14,4** for since they wish Jesus to be really a man, as I said before, and Christ, who came down in the form of a dove, to have come to be in him (as we have already found among other heresies too) [and] to have been joined to him, and Christ himself to be [...] begotten from the seed of a man and of a woman.

**30,14,5a** Yet at other times they deny that he is a man, ostensibly on the strength of the saying the Saviour spoke when it was reported to him, “Behold, your mother and your brothers are standing outside”, namely: “Who are my mother and brothers?

**30,14,5b** And stretching out his hand towards the disciples he said: ‘These are my brothers and mother and sisters, who do the will of my Father.’”

**30,14,6** From this, as I have said, Ebion, filled with every kind of trickery, shows himself in many forms, so as to be a monstrosity, in keeping with what I have already set out above.

**30,15,1** They also make use of some other books — the so-called Travels of Peter, supposedly, written by Clement — falsifying their contents, though leaving a few true things,

**30,15,2a** as Clement himself refutes them on every point from the encyclical letters he wrote, which are read in the holy churches, in that his faith and his discourse have a different character from the things falsified in his name by these people in the Travels.

**30,15,2b** For he teaches virginity, and they do not accept it; he praises Elijah and David and Samson and all the prophets, whom these men abhor.

**30,15,3** So in the Travels they have turned everything to suit themselves, telling lies against Peter in many ways — that he was baptized every day for the sake of purification, as these men are; and they say that he abstained from animate creatures and from meats, as they themselves do, and from every other food prepared from flesh, since Ebion himself and the Ebionites abstain from these things entirely.

**30,15,4** And if you ask one of them for what reason they do not partake of animate creatures, having nothing to explain, they answer senselessly and say, “We do not partake of them because they come from intercourse and the mingling of bodies” — so that they themselves, who come from the mingling of man and woman, are wholly abominable, by the very words their own mouth has foolishly vomited out.

**30,16,1a** They too receive baptism, apart from the baptisms they undergo every day.

**30,16,1b** And they celebrate mysteries, supposedly, in imitation of the holy things in the church, from year to year, using unleavened bread, and for the other part of the mystery, water alone.

**30,16,2a** And they hold, as I said, that two were appointed by God, the one Christ and the other the devil.

**30,16,2b** And they say that Christ has received the lot of the age to come, while the devil has been entrusted with this age, by order, supposedly, of the Almighty, as each of them requested.

**30,16,3** And for this reason they say that Jesus was born from a man’s seed and was chosen, and so by election was called Son of God, on account of the Christ who came into him from above in the form of a dove.

**30,16,4** But they do not say that he was begotten of God the Father, but that he was created like one of the archangels, and yet is lord both of angels and of all [the things] made by the Almighty, and that he came and taught [...],

**30,16,5a** as the gospel so called among them contains: “I have come to do away with the sacrifices, and unless you cease from sacrificing, the wrath will not cease from you.”

**30,16,5b** These and certain things like them are the crafty devices among them.

**30,16,6** And they call certain other works Acts of the Apostles, in which much is full of impiety; and from these they have armed themselves against the truth with no small effort.

**30,16,7** And they put forward certain Ascents and instructions, supposedly, in the Ascents of James, as though he were declaring against the temple and the sacrifices, and against the fire upon the altar, and many other things full of empty babble;

**30,16,8** so that here too they are not ashamed to accuse Paul with certain fabricated words, composed out of the knavery and error of their false apostles: they call him a Tarsian, as he himself acknowledges and does not deny, but they make him out to be of Greek birth, taking their pretext from the passage where he said, out of love for the truth, “I am a Tarsian, a citizen of no obscure city.”

**30,16,9a** Then they say that he was a Greek, the son of a Greek mother and a Greek father, and that he went up to Jerusalem and stayed there for a time, and that he desired to take the daughter of the priest in marriage and for this reason became a proselyte and was circumcised.

**30,16,9b** And when after all he did not get such a girl, he grew angry and wrote against circumcision, the sabbath and the law.

**30,17,1a** But in every way this dreadful serpent, poor in understanding as he is, deals in false accusation.

**30,17,1b** For ‘Ebion’, rendered from the Hebrew into the Greek tongue, means ‘poor’.

**30,17,1c** For truly poor in understanding and in hope and in deed is [he who] considered Christ a mere man and so has held his hope concerning him in a poverty of faith.

**30,17,2** But they, supposedly, pride themselves, calling themselves poor because, as they say, in the times of the apostles they sold their possessions and laid them at the feet of the apostles and went over to poverty and renunciation; and for this reason, he says, they are called ‘poor’ by everyone.

**30,17,3** But not even this is true of them; rather, he was in reality called Ebion by birth, the poor wretch having had the name allotted to him, by prophecy I think, from his father and his mother.

**30,17,4a** And how many other dreadful things, perverted and full of wickedness, are kept up among them?

**30,17,4b** For whenever any of them falls sick or is bitten by a serpent, he goes down into the water and invokes the names that are in Elchasai — of heaven and earth, of salt and water, of the winds and the angels of righteousness, [as] they say, and of bread and oil — and begins to say: “Help me and rid me of the pain.”

**30,17,5** But I have already shown above that Ebion knew nothing of these things; in time, however, those who came from him, having joined themselves to Elchasai, have kept Ebion’s circumcision, sabbath and customs, but Elchasai’s fantasy,

**30,17,6a** so that they think Christ is a kind of man-shaped figure, invisible to men, ninety-six miles in length, supposedly twenty-four schoeni, and six schoeni in breadth, twenty-four miles, and in thickness according to some other measurement.

**30,17,6b** And that opposite him the Holy Spirit too stands, invisibly, in the form of a female, and is itself of the same measure.

**30,17,7a** “And how,” he says, “did I come to know the measurements?

**30,17,7b** Because,” he says, “I observed from the mountains that their heads came up to them, and by learning the measure of the mountain I came to know the measurements of Christ and of the Holy Spirit.”

**30,17,8a** I have already spoken about these things in the heresy against the Ossaeans.

**30,17,8b** But I have set these things down now in passing, so that what exists in each nation and heresy, but is found among others as well, may not be thought to have been left unmentioned by us through forgetfulness.

**30,18,1** This Ebion, then, himself also had his preaching in Asia and in Rome, but they have the roots of the thorny offshoots chiefly from Nabataea and Paneas, and from Moabitis and Cocaba in the land of Bashan beyond Adraa, and also in Cyprus.

**30,18,2** And they compel the young and give them in marriage before their time, supposedly on the authority of their teachers (for these people have elders and synagogue-rulers, and the name they give their church is ‘synagogue’, not ‘church’, and they pride themselves on the name of Christ alone)

**30,18,3** and they permit unions to be made not only within one marriage, but if anyone should wish to be separated from his first marriage and be joined to another, they permit it — for everything is allowed among them without restraint — as far as a second marriage, and a third, and even a seventh.

**30,18,4a** They acknowledge Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Moses, Aaron, and Joshua son of Nun — but simply as the successor of Moses, of no account in himself.

**30,18,4b** But after these they no longer acknowledge any of the prophets, but even anathematise and mock David and Solomon; likewise Isaiah, Jeremiah, Daniel, Ezekiel, Elijah and Elisha they set aside, for they do not assent to them, blaspheming their prophecies, and they accept only the gospel.

**30,18,5** And they call Christ a prophet of the truth, and Christ, [but] Son of God by advancement and by conjunction with the exaltation that came to him from above; and the prophets, they say, are prophets of [...] understanding and not of truth.

**30,18,6** And they wish him alone to be both prophet and man and Son of God and Christ, and a mere man, as we said before, who through virtue of life came to be called Son of God.

**30,18,7a** Nor do they accept the whole Pentateuch of Moses, but reject certain passages.

**30,18,7b** And when you say to them concerning the eating of animate creatures, “How then did Abraham set the calf and the milk before the angels?

**30,18,7c** Or how did Noah eat, and hear from God saying, ‘Slay and eat’, and how did Isaac and Jacob sacrifice to God, and likewise Moses in the wilderness?”, he disbelieves those things and says, “What need have I to read what is in the law, now that the gospel has come?”

**30,18,8a** “Where, then, did you get your knowledge of Moses and Abraham?

**30,18,8b** For I know that you acknowledge that they existed, and you enrol them as righteous men and as your own fathers.”

**30,18,9** Then he answers and says, “Christ revealed it to me”, and blasphemes the greater part of the legislation, and Samson and David and Elijah and Samuel and Elisha and the others.

**30,19,1** But the charlatan is refuted on every side by the Saviour, who expressly and, as it were, in brief, from a single saying, refutes the whole of his deceitful teaching, saying: “John came in the way of righteousness, eating nothing and drinking nothing, and they say, ‘He has a demon’; the Son of Man has come eating and drinking, [and they say, ‘Look, a man who is a glutton and a wine-drinker’].”

**30,19,2** And it is by no means that John did not eat even what came to hand, or that the Saviour ate anything whatever, so that one might take it to include even forbidden things;

**30,19,3** but the Scripture makes plain the sense of the truth: his being “a glutton and a wine-drinker” is nothing other than eating meat and drinking wine; and “not eating nor drinking” means that John took no share of meat or wine, but partook only of locusts and honey — and plainly of water too.

**30,19,4a** And who does not know that the Saviour, after he had risen from the dead, also ate?

**30,19,4b** As the holy gospels of the truth have it: “Bread and a portion of broiled fish were given to him, and he took and ate, and gave to the disciples”; just as he did by the sea of Tiberias too, where he both ate and gave.

**30,19,5a** Much indeed might be said on this point.

**30,19,5b** But I must come in detail to the refutation of these stale and rotten words that are preached among them, and accomplish their overthrow.

**30,20,1a** And first, concerning Christ, it must be said that he is not a mere man.

**30,20,1b** For one born in all respects [as] a man cannot be granted to the world as a sign, as the Holy Spirit prophesied of him when he said to Ahaz, “Ask a sign for yourself”; and when he did not ask, then the prophet says, “The Lord himself shall give you a sign: see, the virgin will be with child.”

**30,20,2** But a woman who has been joined to a man and married is not called a virgin; rather, she would properly be called a virgin who in truth conceived God the Word without a man,

**30,20,3a** as Isaiah himself says in another place: “A voice of outcry out of the city, a voice out of the temple, the voice of the Lord of recompense, repaying his adversaries: before she who was in labour gave birth, before the pain of her pangs came, she escaped it and bore a male.

**30,20,3b** Who has heard such a thing, or who has seen such things?

**30,20,3c** Did the land travail in a single day, and bear a nation all at once?

**30,20,3d** For Zion travailed and bore her children; but I gave this expectation, and they did not remember it, says the Lord.”

**30,20,4** And what expectation, and what children, but that a virgin should give birth without pangs, which has happened nowhere, and that John, the child born for his sake from Elizabeth according to promise, [...], even though John was born through pangs?

**30,20,5a** How then do these people define the Saviour as a mere man from the seed of a man?

**30,20,5b** And how will it not be recognised, as Jeremiah too says of him, that “He is a man, and who will know him?”

**30,20,6a** For the prophet, in giving an account of him, said, “Who will know him?”

**30,20,6b** Had he been speaking of a mere man, his father and his mother would surely have known him, and his kinsfolk and neighbours, his housemates and fellow citizens;

**30,20,7a** but since what was born is from Mary, while God the Word came from above — not from the seed of a man, but begotten of the Father above in truth, timelessly and without beginning, and in the last days pleased to come to be in the womb of a virgin and from her to fashion flesh for himself — for this reason he says, “And man he is, but who will know him?”

**30,20,7b** For it was God who came from above, the only-begotten God the Word.

**30,20,8** Great is the wretchedness of those who have gone astray, who have abandoned the testimonies of prophets and of angels and have put up with Ebion, the deluded one, who wishes to satisfy his own pleasure and handles the practices of the Jews while being estranged from the Jews.

**30,20,9** [For] Gabriel, bringing the good news at once to the virgin, when she said, “How shall these things be, since I do not know a man?”, set forth the sure ground, saying: “The Spirit of the Lord will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; therefore also the holy one who is born of you shall be called Son of God.”

**30,20,10** For in saying “who is born” he showed that the flesh [was] from her, and the rest of the incarnation, but that from above, from heaven, the power of the Most High and the Holy Spirit cast their shadow over the holy virgin, and that the only-begotten Son, God the Word, came down from above, [...] both how he became man and how he was in truth born of her.

**30,20,11a** And how many other such things are there?

**30,20,11b** But it is my custom, as I promised, not to be carried away at length, so as not to swell the work to a great bulk.

**30,21,1** Next I shall give an account of the other matters, the lies they tell against Peter and the other apostles: that Peter, he says, used baptisms every day before he would even partake of bread.

**30,21,2a** And see the whole of their slander and the festering falseness of their charlatan’s teaching.

**30,21,2b** For because they are defiled and often do many things in a rather lustful manner on the earth, they use water lavishly to persuade themselves of their own assurance, so that, no doubt, they deceive themselves, thinking they have cleansing through baptisms.

**30,21,3** And they are not ashamed to vent these drunken insults upon the apostles, although the Lord refutes their perversity, in that Peter said, when he came to wash his feet, “You shall never wash my feet”, and the reply was given by the Saviour, “If I do not wash your feet, you have no part with me”,

**30,21,4** and he again answered, “Not only the feet, but also the head”, and the Lord again said, “He who has bathed once has no need [to wash] his head, but only his feet; for he is wholly clean.”

**30,21,5a** He showed, then, that one ought not to make use of baptisms and unprofitable customs and the commandments and teachings of men, as in the gospel too, in harmony with the prophet, he says: “This people honours me with the lips, while their heart keeps far away from me.

**30,21,5b** And it is in vain that they worship me, teaching as teachings the commandments of men.”

**30,21,6a** Otherwise, for what reason did he blame the Pharisees and scribes, who practised baptisms with the fist, of [both] themselves and of jugs and cups and the rest?

**30,21,6b** And why does he declare outright that “eating with unwashed hands does not defile a man”?

**30,21,6c** So he not only overturned their baptizing, but also showed that washing the hands is superfluous, and that there is no harm to one who does [not] wish to wash his hands.

**30,22,1a** And how will their folly concerning the eating of meat not be refuted at once?

**30,22,1b** First, by the fact that the Lord ate the Passover of the Jews.

**30,22,1c** And the Passover of the Jews was a sheep and unleavened bread, the flesh of a sheep roasted with fire and eaten;

**30,22,2a** as the disciples also say to him, “Where do you wish us to prepare for you to eat the Passover?”

**30,22,2b** And the Lord himself says, “Go into the city, and you will find a man carrying a jar of water; follow him where he goes, and say to the master of the house, ‘Where is the lodging in which I am to keep the Passover with my disciples?’

**30,22,2c** And he will show you an upper room spread with couches; prepare there.”

**30,22,3a** Again, the Lord himself says, “With longing I have longed to eat this Passover with you”; and not simply ‘Passover’ did he say, but ‘this Passover’, so that no one might craftily turn it to his own notion.

**30,22,3b** And the Passover, as I have said, consisted of meat roasted in the fire, and the other things.

**30,22,4** But they, having done away for themselves with the sequence of the truth, altered the passage — which is plain to all from the words joined to it — and made the disciples say, “Where do you wish us to prepare for you to eat the Passover?”, and him, supposedly, say, “I have not longed with longing to eat meat this Passover with you.”

**30,22,5a** And how will their knavery not be detected, when the sequence cries out that the mu and the eta are additions?

**30,22,5b** For instead of saying “With longing I have longed”, they added the adverb “not”.

**30,22,5c** But he in truth used to say, “With longing I have longed to eat this Passover with you”; whereas they, by writing in ‘meat’, have led themselves astray, playing the knave and saying, “I have not longed with longing to eat meat this Passover with you”; but it is clearly shown that he both kept the Passover and ate meat, as I said before.

**30,22,6** But they will also be refuted by the vision revealed to the holy Peter through the sheet that held within it all wild beasts and cattle and creeping things and birds, and by the voice of the Lord saying, “Rise, slay and eat”; and when Peter said, “By no means, Lord; nothing common or unclean has entered my mouth”, the Lord says, “What God has cleansed, you must not call common.”

**30,22,7a** For the establishing of the truth will be found in two ways.

**30,22,7b** For if they say that [the] saying of the holy Peter, when he said, “Nothing common or unclean ever [entered my mouth]”, extended to all foods, so that oxen and goats and sheep and birds were called common by him, they will at once be refuted by his former manner of life.

**30,22,8a** For it was only after he had married, had children of his own and had a mother-in-law that he came to meet the Saviour, being by origin of the Jews.

**30,22,8b** Now Jews eat flesh, and with them meat-eating is neither abhorred nor forbidden.

**30,22,9a** So then, since he had eaten from the beginning — even if we should say until he met the Saviour — it will be shown that he considered nothing common among the things not called common.

**30,22,9b** For he did not apply ‘common and unclean’ to all things, but to those which the law called common and unclean.

**30,22,10** Again, once this has been shown — that his concern was not with all meats, as though all were common, but with those that were common and unclean in the law — God, handing down the character of the holy church of Christ, said to him that he should regard nothing as common: “for everything is clean when received with thanksgiving and the blessing of God.”

**30,22,11** But even if the riddle had to do with the calling of the Gentiles, so that he should not consider those in uncircumcision common or unclean, still Peter’s statement carried no thought of human beings, but of the things forbidden in the law, as would be clear to anyone; and their foolish argument has collapsed on every side.

**30,23,1** They feignedly accept the names of the apostles, to persuade those who have been deceived by them, and they have fabricated books in their name and written them up, supposedly in the person of James and Matthew and other disciples.

**30,23,2a** Among these names they include the name of John the apostle too, so that their folly may be exposed on every side.

**30,23,2b** For he not only refutes them on every count when he says, “In the beginning the Word existed, and the Word existed with God, and the Word was God”,

**30,23,3** but also because the testimonies of the holy prophets [...], in which, moved by the Holy Spirit and giving a fitting and exact account of what was said by the Saviour, he set out, as I said, the testimonies concerning each matter fulfilled in Christ — those prophets from whom these men have estranged themselves.

**30,23,4** And at once he showed how John himself answers those sent to John the Baptist by the Pharisees, saying, “I am a voice of one calling out in the desert: ‘Straighten the Lord’s way’, as Isaiah the prophet said.”

**30,23,5a** And again John himself, drawing his testimony from the prophets — from David, I mean — says, at the time when the Lord upset the money-changers’ tables and said, “Do not make my Father’s house a house of trade”: “They remembered that it was written, ‘Zeal for your house has devoured me.’”

**30,23,5b** Once more John himself said, “Isaiah saw while he was in the Holy Spirit.”

**30,24,1a** Again, the same holy John, while preaching in Asia, is recorded to have done a remarkable deed as a pattern of the truth.

**30,24,1b** For though he led a most wonderful life, befitting the dignity of his apostleship, and did not bathe at all, he was constrained by the Holy Spirit to go out to the bath-house, saying, “Get me what I need for the bath.”

**30,24,2** And while those who followed him wondered, he came to the bath itself, and going up to the man whose custom it was to take charge of the bathers’ clothes, he asked who was inside in the bath-house;

**30,24,3** and the attendant, [he] who was occupied in guarding the clothes (for in the gymnasia some have this work in order to earn their daily food), told the holy John that Ebion was inside.

**30,24,4** And John, at once understanding the guidance of the Holy Spirit — the reason for which it had urged him to come to the bath-house, as I said, for a memorial, to leave us the pattern of the truth, showing who are the servants of Christ and apostles and sons of the truth itself, and what are the vessels of the evil one and the gates of Hades, which do not prevail against the rock and the holy church of God built upon it — at once was troubled and groaned,

**30,24,5** and calmly, so that all might hear, in testimony to the establishing of undefiled teaching, “Make haste, brothers,” he said, “let us go out from here, lest the bath-house fall and bury us with Ebion, who is inside the bath-house, on account of his impiety.”

**30,24,6a** And let no one be surprised on hearing that Ebion met John.

**30,24,6b** For the blessed John, remaining long in this life, lasted until the reign of Trajan.

**30,24,7** And that all the apostles reject the faith of Ebion and considered it alien to the character of their preaching is clear to everyone.

**30,25,1a** And as for the holy Paul, how they speak of him in blasphemy — how much have I to say?

**30,25,1b** For first they say that he was a Greek and of Gentile origin, and that he became a proselyte later.

**30,25,2** How then does he say of himself, “A Hebrew born of Hebrews, from Abraham’s seed, of Benjamin’s tribe, a Pharisee as regards the law, being far more zealous for the traditions of my fathers”;

**30,25,3** and in another place he says, “Israelites they are, so am I; Abraham’s seed they are, so am I”; and he says, “Circumcised on the eighth day, and reared at the feet of Gamaliel, and a Hebrew born of Hebrews”?

**30,25,4a** Oh, the dreadful shrieks of the dreadful reptiles, and their dragon-like hissings, and the poisons of their empty babble!

**30,25,4b** And whose testimony shall I accept?

**30,25,4c** That of Ebion and his followers, or that of the holy Peter, who says, “As my brother Paul wrote to you — things which are deep and hard to interpret, which the unlearned and unstable twist in their own ignorance”?

**30,25,5a** And again the holy Paul himself bears witness to Peter and his companions, saying, “James and John and Cephas, who were reputed to be pillars, gave to me and to Barnabas the right hands of fellowship.”

**30,25,5b** For even if he should call himself a Tarsian, this by no means gives a pretext to those who hunt for words, to their own ruin and that of those who are persuaded [...], for holding that it is so.

**30,25,6a** For he also speaks of Barnabas — once called Joseph but renamed Barnabas, which is translated ‘son of consolation’ — as a Levite, a Cypriot by birth.

**30,25,6b** And it does not at all follow that because he was a Cypriot he was not of Levi; just as the holy Paul too, even if he came from Tarsus, was no stranger to Israel.

**30,25,7** For when many were scattered in the times of Antiochus Epiphanes and at other times, whenever war broke out, and were taken captive and given over to siege, the captives who were carried off remained in certain places, while others, withdrawing for such a reason, settled each wherever he had arrived.

**30,25,8** As the holy Jeremiah too said concerning Israel — since this very thing, fleeing before the face of the enemy, has often happened — “Even if you go away to the Kittim, not even there will you have rest.”

**30,25,9a** And it is clear to everyone that the island of the Cypriots is called Kition; for the Kitians are Cypriots and Rhodians.

**30,25,9b** But the race of the Cypriots and Rhodians has also settled in Macedonia; from there indeed Alexander the Macedonian came.

**30,25,9c** And for this reason it is stated in the Maccabees that “he came out from the land of the Kittim”; for Alexander the Macedonian was of the seed of the Kittim.

**30,25,10** But, returning again to the subject — having gone through the history of these matters because of the topic that came up — I say that after the deportation many who settled in the other lands were of the seed of Israel by descent.

**30,25,11** For they were also called natives of each land, just as the daughters of Jethro reported Moses’ help to their father, how he drove off the shepherds and watered their sheep, and having gone they told their own father about it, and when he said, “Why have you hastened to come today?”

**30,25,12** They said to him, “An Egyptian man rescued us from the shepherds, and drew water and watered our flock”, and at once he said to them, “Why did you not bring him here, so that he might eat bread?”

**30,25,13a** And who does not know that Moses is the son of Amram and Jochebed, Amram the son of Kohath, Kohath son of Levi, Levi son of Jacob, Jacob son of Isaac, and Isaac son of Abraham?

**30,25,13b** And the sequence of his noble birth and of his seed by no means fell away because Moses was called an Egyptian.

**30,25,14** But these who have strayed from Ebion, having left the way, have given their mind over to many crooked ways and an uphill path.

**30,26,1** Again, they boast of having circumcision, and pride themselves that this is, supposedly, the seal and mark of the patriarchs and of the righteous who lived according to the law, through which they think they are made equal to them; and indeed they wish to bring the proof of it from Christ himself, as Cerinthus and his followers do too.

**30,26,2** For these too say, following the foolish argument of those people, “It is enough for the disciple to be as the teacher”; “Christ was circumcised,” he says, “so you too be circumcised.”

**30,26,3** [...] and so that the seeds of error may be shamed on every side; just as a bridle was set for the sea by God — bars and gates, sand as a boundary for the sea, and the command, “Thus far shall you come, and you shall not pass beyond, but your waves shall be broken within you”, [as] it says — so these too will be consumed within themselves.

**30,26,4** But there the word of the boundary was spoken by God, by God’s command, for the good order of the sea; here, however, wickedness and error, of its own accord, blinding the mind and turning away from devout reasoning, has been first to raise up waves, as it were, against itself, dashing the wretched notions it had proclaimed beforehand against other waves of its own, and ever being broken within itself [and] dissolving itself —

**30,26,5** or like a dreadful reptile that injures itself, turning back upon itself from the tail and devouring itself, becoming its own consumer.

**30,26,6a** For they say that this once happened with asps shut up in jars: each consumed another, and the boldest and fiercest remained to the end; [and], left alone and coming to hunger, it began from its own tail and devoured itself, as certain naturalists relate, in the country of the Egyptians.

**30,26,6b** Hence they also fittingly gave it its name, on account of the Gorgon’s head, calling it too an asp-gorgon.

**30,26,7** So too the vain-minded Ebion and those around him have been first to cut themselves to pieces, destroying from the beginning the very things on which they pride themselves.

**30,26,8** For Christ did not circumcise himself, born as an infant; but glory to the merciful God that Ebion has outrun himself, so as not to confess the truth, so that this too might become a refutation of him.

**30,26,9a** For if he said that he, being God come from above, had been circumcised with the eighth-day [circumcision] by Mary, then (since he was God and consented by his own good pleasure) he would have afforded the charlatan a plausible argument about circumcision.

**30,26,9b** But since he introduces the idea that he was born a mere man from human beings, the child is no longer responsible, even if he was circumcised with the eighth-day circumcision.

**30,26,10a** For it was not he who circumcised himself; he was circumcised at the hands of men.

**30,26,10b** For children do not circumcise themselves, nor are they responsible for their own circumcision, but their parents are.

**30,26,10c** For they, being without knowledge, are infants in their ignorance and do not even know what is being done to them by their parents.

**30,27,1** But we say that he is also God, who came from heaven and remained in the womb of the virgin Mary for the time that children are carried in the womb, so that he might fully accomplish for himself, from the virgin womb, the incarnation in the flesh and the dispensation in which he was also truly circumcised, and not in semblance, on the eighth day.

**30,27,2** For “he came to fulfil the law and the prophets, and not to destroy”, not to declare the law alien, but as given by himself and as having remained in types until him, so that in him and through him what was lacking in the law might again be fulfilled, so that the types, arriving at their spiritual completion, might be proclaimed in truth by him and by his apostles, being no longer types but truth.

**30,27,3** For in him was fulfilled what was spoken in the law, which lasted until his time and in him ceased, passing over into fulfilment — as Zipporah said: “The blood of my child’s circumcision has stopped.”

**30,27,4** And she did not say, “I have circumcised my child”; for the angel who was sent was not arranging that a circumcision should take place, nor did he withdraw from fear of the blood of the circumcision, but, as a reminder of the child who was to come, to stop the blood of circumcision [...]; and having heard this and so arranged it, he withdrew.

**30,27,5** And of what child — listen — but the one of whom the prophet said, “They will wish that they had been burned with fire; for a child has been born to us, a son also has been given to us”,

**30,27,6** showing the child born in truth, to signify the true incarnation, and with “a son has been given to us”, to show that the Word of God from above and Son of God was himself given and, coming to be in the womb, became man, being both, God and man: himself God, himself man, himself the Son given from above, himself the child that was born.

**30,27,7** With him, then, the blood of circumcision stopped, as it says in the gospel too, when Greeks came up to see him and approached Philip, saying to him, “Show us Jesus”; then Philip spoke to John and John to Jesus: “Some Greeks wish to see you”,

**30,27,8** and at once the Lord answers, saying, “Now the glory of God has come”, to show that the circumcision in the flesh was passing away, having served as a type for a time, while uncircumcision in the flesh, having in the spirit a greater circumcision, beholds Christ and has truly apprehended him.

**30,28,1** But if these people wish to say, “How then was Christ circumcised?”, we have already shown you, deluded ones, for what reason he was circumcised.

**30,28,2a** For he was circumcised for many reasons.

**30,28,2b** And first, to show [that he had taken] flesh in truth, because of Mani and those who say that he appeared in semblance;

**30,28,3** next, to show that the body was not of one substance with the Godhead, as Apollinarius claims, nor did he bring it down from above, as Valentinus says;

**30,28,4a** and to ratify the circumcision he had given long before, which had served rightly until his coming, and so that the Jews might have no excuse.

**30,28,4b** Had he not been circumcised, they would have been able to say, “We could not accept a Christ in uncircumcision.”

**30,28,5** And besides, since he had commanded Abraham to be circumcised for the sake of a visible seal, given as a type of the true and invisible one, Christ had to confirm it by being circumcised.

**30,28,6** For the visible circumcision arose because of Abraham’s hesitation, when that holy and righteous man said, as one in doubt, “Will a son be born to a man of a hundred years?” and “Will Sarah, the old woman, bear a son?”, and at once the Lord said, “Take me a ram of three years and a goat and a heifer”, and so on, and about the setting of the sun, when he saw torches of fire and an oven and the rest,

**30,28,7** God, having said to him by way of rebuke, “Your seed shall be a sojourner in a land not its own, and they shall enslave it four hundred years”, laid upon him and upon them the circumcision in the flesh for safety’s sake, because of the doubt through which he had said, “Will a son be born to a man of a hundred years?”, so that the seed, enslaved by idolatrous and unbelieving Egyptians, might not forget the God of its fathers, and so that, seeing it, they might be reminded and put to shame and not deny him.

**30,28,8** And this lasted until Christ, and for this reason he too was pleased to be circumcised, and became man in truth, coming from above from the Father as God the Word, not laying aside the Godhead but wearing flesh in truth;

**30,28,9** and having a complete incarnation he is circumcised, ordering all things in truth, so that the Jews, as I said before, might be without excuse, and the Manichaeans and others will be refuted, and so that, having been circumcised, he might with good reason abolish circumcision and point to another, greater one — not as one who lacked it and was commending himself, but as one who had it and showed that there is no longer need of this, but of the greater.

**30,29,1a** The magi will plainly show that he was God from the moment of his birth, and not a mere man.

**30,29,1b** For after a space of two years — as they had shown Herod the time of the star, “from two years old and under” — these same magi, having come to Jerusalem and inquired, and having learned that the Christ is born in Bethlehem, set out again with the star guiding them and went from Jerusalem to Bethlehem.

**30,29,2** And entering, they found him with his mother Mary, and falling down they worshipped him and offered their gifts.

**30,29,3** If, then, he is worshipped at once, the one born is not a mere man but God, and he does not become Christ after thirty years or after the baptism, but was born at once from a virgin as Christ, God and man.

**30,29,4** Therefore the angels too at once sing praise, saying, “Glory in the highest to God, and peace on earth, among men good pleasure”, and bring the good news to the shepherds, saying, “There has been born to you today Christ the Lord, in the city of David.”

**30,29,5** And not only is this shown, deluded Ebion, but from the age of twelve he is also found “seated among the priests and elders, asking them questions and inquiring with them”, and “they were astonished at the word of grace that proceeded out of his mouth.”

**30,29,6** And he did not do this after his thirtieth year, so that you might say that he became Christ when the Spirit came into him, but at once from the age of twelve, as I said before, as the Gospel according to Luke records.

**30,29,7** But even earlier still, when he was a child, Joseph and Mary went up (it says) to Jerusalem at the feast to worship; and as they were returning, Jesus stayed behind, and they sought him on the journey and among their kinsfolk and did not find him (for Mary had kindred there);

**30,29,8a** and turning back and finding him, she said to him, “Why have you done this to us, child?

**30,29,8b** Behold, I and your father have been seeking you in sorrow” (for Joseph stood to him in the place of a father, since he was not his father by nature).

**30,29,9a** Then the Lord says to her, “Why is it that you were seeking me?

**30,29,9b** Did you not know that I must be in the house of my Father?”, meaning that the temple had been built for God’s name — the name, that is, of his own Father.

**30,29,10** If, then, from infancy he knew the temple and the Father, Jesus who was born was not a mere man, nor was he called Son and Christ only once his thirtieth year was past, when the dove’s form had come into him; but at once, and quite boldly, he taught that he must be in his Father’s house.

**30,29,11a** And that Joseph was not his father, but [stood] in the place of a father, hear from the same evangelist, who wrote in the person of Mary, “I and your father have been seeking you in sorrow”; again, how he writes, saying, “And Jesus, when he began, was about thirty years old, being the son, as was supposed, of Joseph.”

**30,29,11b** And in saying “as was supposed” he showed that he was not in fact his son, but was thought to be so.

**30,30,1** But time will fail me as I recount these things for the establishing of the truth and for the refutation of the folly of Ebion and of his falsified school of folly.

**30,30,2a** For how would it not be clear that Joseph was not the father, but was considered to stand in the place of a father?

**30,30,2b** “Behold,” it says, “the virgin will be with child and will bear a son”;

**30,30,3a** and it did not say, “Behold, the woman.”

**30,30,3b** And again in another place it says, “And the heifer shall give birth, and they shall say, ‘She has not given birth.’”

**30,30,3c** For since some of the Manichaeans and Marcionites say that he was not born, this is why the text has, “She shall give birth, and they shall say, ‘She has not given birth’”; since Mary did not give birth from the seed of a man, and these people in their madness falsely say that she gave birth from the seed of a man.

**30,30,3d** So the heifer in truth gave birth to God, to man in truth.

**30,30,4** And that the virgin is called a heifer, and that the remains of the heifer were a purification for those who had been defiled, hear the law saying, “Take for yourself a flame-red heifer”, to show the chosen vessel of Mary [...] because of the fiery nature of the Saviour’s Godhead contained in the virgin; for “God”, it says, “is a consuming fire.”

**30,30,5** And the law says, “a flame-red heifer, on which no yoke has come upon the neck”, to show that the virgin is a heifer who has not known the yoke of marriage to a man.

**30,30,6a** But why should I discuss so much?

**30,30,6b** Just so Isaiah, speaking again in the Lord’s person, said, “Take for yourself a tome of new, large paper”: a ‘tome’, because the virgin is of the seed of a man, but has been cut off from intercourse with men and was cut off from the natural custom of human beings.

**30,30,7** For all human beings are born from the seed of a man; but the birth of Christ had its incarnation according to nature from a woman, the virgin Mary, yet contrary to nature it was cut off from the sequence proper to humanity, as Jacob too says concerning him, “From a shoot, my son, you went up”, and he did not say, “From seed you went up”.

**30,30,8** This is why the holy prophet Isaiah says — or rather, it is the Lord who tells him — “Take for yourself a tome of paper”, signifying the form of ordinary usage, in which the sons of men write all things; as in the hundred and thirty-eighth psalm too it says, “In your book shall all be written; days shall be fashioned, and no one among them”; for he likened the womb to a book.

**30,30,9** Therefore David says, “Your eyes saw my unwrought substance”, that is, you knew me when I had been sown, before I was given form, and still earlier, before I was sown.

**30,31,1a** But the Hebrew makes the saying wonderfully clear.

**30,31,1b** For it called the unwrought substance ‘golmi’, which is translated ‘groats’ or ‘a grain of fine flour’, that is, what has not yet come together into bread and been kneaded, but like a little granule cut from a grain of wheat, or groat-meal, or a fine particle, which comes from fine flour.

**30,31,2** Thus, giving an exact account, it has shown the counterpart of the form that is cut off from a man for sowing, and it says, when the word is translated into the Greek language, “my unwrought substance” — that is, what is still unformed in the womb or before the womb — “your eyes saw” (“for God knows all things before they come to be”, as it is written); but the ‘book’ and the ‘tome’ signify the womb.

**30,31,3** And it did not say, “Take for yourself a book” or “Take for yourself paper”, but “a tome”, because of its being set apart from the custom of human beings; “of paper”, because of the womb’s being written upon; “new”, because of the newness of the virgin and her undefiled state.

**30,31,4a** [“Great”]: for great indeed, before God and men, is Mary the holy virgin.

**30,31,4b** For how should we not call her great, who contained the one who cannot be contained, whom heaven and earth cannot contain?

**30,31,4c** Yet he who cannot be contained was contained of his own will and good pleasure, willingly and not under necessity.

**30,31,4d** Therefore the tome is great and new: great because of the wonder, new because she is a virgin.

**30,31,5a** “And write,” it says, “in it with the stylus of a man”, and it did not say, “Someone shall write in it with the stylus of a man”, nor did it say, “A man shall write in it”, so that Ebion might find no pretext.

**30,31,5b** For had it said, “A man shall write in it”, he would have been able to say that Joseph, a man, did the sowing, and that Christ was begotten of a man’s seed.

**30,31,6** But to Isaiah, seven hundred and fifty-three years before, more or less, he said “Write”, so that from the length of the interval the truth might be seen by all, that no one seven hundred and fifty-three years before could sow the one who was to be born.

**30,31,7a** Does he then say “Write” to the prophet in vain?

**30,31,7b** No; it was so that he might show the Holy Spirit in the prophet to be the very one who wrought in truth the conception of Christ’s coming in the flesh; for “the Holy Spirit will come upon you”, the angel Gabriel says to Mary, and what follows.

**30,31,8** And “with the stylus of a man” signifies the likeness of a man; for “a man is Christ Jesus, and mediator between God and men”, since God the Word came from above, but is man from Mary, not begotten of the seed of a man.

**30,31,9** And for this reason the prophet at once says, “And he went in to the prophetess”, to show that Mary is the prophetess, and not the wife of Ahaz, as some mistakenly say, claiming that it was spoken with reference to Hezekiah.

**30,31,10a** For by then Hezekiah was already eleven years old.

**30,31,10b** For it was in his father’s third regnal year that the saying “Behold, the virgin will be with child” was prophesied.

**30,31,10c** And when Ahaz had died, having reigned fourteen years and come to his end, the Scripture immediately says, “And Hezekiah became king, and he was twenty-[five] years old when he became king.”

**30,31,11** How then, in the reign of his father, who reigned fourteen years, when Emmanuel, to be born of a virgin, was being prophesied, will Hezekiah be born, he who reigned at twenty years of age after his father; and will it not rather be evident to the intelligent that he had already been born when the prophet foretold the word in the reign of Ahaz, the father of Hezekiah?

**30,31,12a** Especially since the wife of Ahaz was not a prophetess either, as is clear to everyone.

**30,31,12b** But Mary is, she who said and prophesied, “From now on all the generations of the earth shall call me blessed”; to whom Gabriel came in and brought the good news that the Spirit who spoke in Isaiah would come upon her, and that she would give birth to a son, our Lord Jesus Christ, by the Holy Spirit and not of a man’s seed, as these people, in their senselessness and error, blasphemously claim.

**30,32,1a** The sabbath-keeping of this vain-minded man has also been refuted, and his circumcision, and the daily baptisms he performs; for Jesus makes a point of doing most of his healing on the sabbath.

**30,32,1b** And not only does he heal, but he heals in two ways.

**30,32,2** For he bids those healed on the sabbath take up their bed and walk; and besides, on the sabbath he kneaded clay and put it on the eyes of the blind man; and to knead clay is work.

**30,32,3a** Hence the apostles, knowing from his living and teaching among them that the sabbath had been loosed, pluck ears of grain on the sabbath and rub them and eat.

**30,32,3b** And it was the second-first sabbath, as the gospel has it.

**30,32,4** For the law appointed different sabbaths: the one, the sabbath proper, recurring week by week; the other, on account of the days falling at the new moon by the lunar reckoning and the feasts in their order, such as the days of Tabernacles and the days of the Passover, when they sacrifice the sheep and then eat unleavened bread; and further when they keep the one fast of the year, called the great, and the other, which they call the little.

**30,32,5** For when these days occur, be it the second day of the week, the third or the fourth, that day too is fixed for them as a sabbath.

**30,32,6** Therefore, after the day of unleavened bread had come and been appointed as a sabbath day, on the natural sabbath that followed the day of unleavened bread reckoned as a sabbath, they were found going through the cornfields, and they plucked ears of grain and rubbed them and ate,

**30,32,7** showing that the bond appointed in the sabbath had been loosed, since the great sabbath had come, that is, Christ, who has brought us rest from our sins; of whom Noah was a type, whom his father, seeing him born, named Noah by prophecy, saying, “This one shall give us rest from our sins, that is, from our hard works.”

**30,32,8** But Noah did not give rest from sins; rather, Lamech uttered the prophecy with reference to Christ, who in truth is interpreted ‘Noah’ (for Noah is interpreted ‘rest’) and ‘Sebeth’, which is interpreted ‘rest’ and ‘sabbath’;

**30,32,9a** and this is Christ, in whom the Father has taken his rest, and his Holy Spirit; and all holy men have rested in him, ceasing from sins.

**30,32,9b** He is the great and eternal sabbath, of which the small and temporary sabbath was a type, serving until his coming, ordained by him according to the law, and in him loosed and fulfilled in the gospel; for so he declared: “The Son of Man is Lord of the sabbath as well.”

**30,32,10a** Hence the disciples boldly loose the sabbath, since the priests before them also loosed the sabbath in the temple, sacrificing and offering sacrifices to God, so that the continual sacrifice offered every day might not cease.

**30,32,10b** And it was not only the priests themselves who, by not resting, prophesied the loosing of the sabbath; circumcision itself also loosed the sabbath.

**30,32,11a** For since a child was often born on a sabbath, an annulling of sabbath and of circumcision came about; thus the loosing of both was foretold.

**30,32,11b** For it is shown that those who circumcise the very child born on the sabbath, if they wish to keep the eighth day exactly, find it falling on a sabbath, and by circumcising they have done work and loosed the sabbath;

**30,32,12** but should they postpone it to avoid loosing the sabbath, they then perform the circumcision on the ninth day, and have loosed circumcision itself and the rule of the commandment concerning it, that of eight days.

**30,33,1** But neither was the first circumcision perfect; it was given for a sign, both as a reminder for those who came after and on account of the doubt of the holy Abraham, who, as has been said, was rebuked, and as a type of the great circumcision which fulfils all things equally in those who are counted worthy.

**30,33,2a** For if that circumcision existed for the sake of sanctification and for inheriting the kingdom of heaven, then Sarah has been robbed of the kingdom, and so have Rebecca, Leah, Rachel, Jochebed, Moses’ sister Miriam, and all the holy women, and they will be unable to inherit the heavenly kingdom, not having been able to receive Abraham’s circumcision, which God bestowed on him — according to these people’s argument.

**30,33,2b** But if these have not been shut out of heaven’s kingdom, though they had no share in circumcision, then the present circumcision in the flesh has no force at all.

**30,33,3a** And why does Ebion boast of circumcision, when the idolaters too, and the priests of the Egyptians, have circumcision?

**30,33,3b** Moreover, the Saracens, who are also Ishmaelites, have circumcision, and the Samaritans and Idumaeans and Homerites.

**30,33,3c** Most of them, however, do it not by reason of a law but out of some unreasoning custom.

**30,33,4** And in short, I shall spend a long time wearing myself out over his nonsense, because he leans in vain on the saying which the Saviour spoke, “It is enough for the disciple to be as the teacher”, so that he too may pride himself on his own circumcision on the strength of the circumcision of Christ, which was rightly performed in him and abolished through him.

**30,33,5** Nevertheless, since this brutish man wishes to take this saying as concerning imitation of Christ, I shall not shrink from showing that this is not why it was said.

**30,33,6a** For the Lord at once makes it clear, declaring that it was not for this reason that he spoke it, but on account of the persecutions and the drunken violence of the Jews against him; and he says, “If they persecuted me, they will persecute you too; if they hated me, they will hate you too.”

**30,33,6b** “Do you not call me Lord and Teacher?

**30,33,6c** And you say well, for so I am”; “If, then, they called the master of the house Beelzebul, how much more those of his household?”

**30,33,7** And: “A slave cannot rank above his lord, and no disciple stands above his teacher; rather, let the disciple be made complete in every respect, as the teacher” — that is, ready for persecution and slander and anything whatever that is brought upon him.

**30,33,8a** Hence the holy Paul too said, “Become imitators of me, just as I am of Christ.”

**30,33,8b** And this does not mean that he imitated his Master in a way he ought not; for he did not claim, “I am God”, or “I am God’s Son”, or “I am the Word, God”; rather his words are, “I am the least of the apostles”, adding, “as to one untimely born, he was seen by me also.”

**30,34,1a** But if you, Ebion, take this saying as concerning imitation of Christ, and wish, from the circumcision [about] which you hold your vain notions, to be as the teacher, or rather as the Lord, do not liken yourself to him in circumcision; for it will not profit you.

**30,34,1b** For the Lord has brought its time to a close, as we have clearly shown through many testimonies.

**30,34,2** For he came and fulfilled it, giving the perfect circumcision of his mysteries, not in one member only, but sealing and circumcising the whole body from sin, and saving not one part of the people, that is, men only, but sealing the whole people of the Christians in truth, men and women together, and [...] ungrudgingly for the inheritance of heaven’s kingdom; not deficiently providing the seal, in weakness, to one order alone, that of men, but through the seal, the commandments and good teaching unveiling the kingdom of heaven to a people entire.

**30,34,3a** But if you wish to be as the Lord, Ebion, that is, as the teacher, you are greatly deceived.

**30,34,3b** Do not liken yourself to him in circumcision, but call Lazarus from the tomb, or raise some other dead man; cleanse lepers, or give sight to the blind, or heal one paralysed from birth, if you can; but you cannot, because you act in the contrary way, held fast by unbelief and by the bonds of the flesh and by ordinances of the law that remain unfulfilled.

**30,34,4a** But even supposing you had been able to do these things — which, given your false belief, you cannot.

**30,34,4b** I do not say [...] as Christ.

**30,34,4c** For you cannot be likened to God, being a perishable and deceived man; but neither can you call upon the name of Christ for divine signs, and even if you call upon it, you effect nothing.

**30,34,5** And even if you did at all succeed in raising up a paralytic, he, having risen through the name of Jesus, would also have received understanding from him, so as not to put up with your sabbath-keeping, but to learn from the name of the one who healed him [...] that “Take up your bed and go to your house on the sabbath.”

**30,34,6** It has already been stated by me how each of them suggests something different about Christ: at one time Ebion himself saying that he was born a mere man from intercourse, at another the Ebionites who came from him saying that the Son possessed a power from God above, and that he puts on Adam and puts him off again from time to time; whose differing opinions we have overthrown in the power of God.

**30,34,7** And why should I linger any longer in salty, tidal places, flooded at one time and dried out at another, from which castings-up of fish often occur, and cause harm to the feet of those who walk the shores of these places (because some of the fish among them are venomous — I mean sting-rays and weevers and sharks and morays, as has already been said)?

**30,34,8** I shall therefore pass by the place again, thanking God that we have turned this heresy too to flight, having refuted it not perfunctorily but with care.

**30,34,9** And let us give ourselves to the others in order, beloved, asking God to be our helper, so that he himself may bring to completion through us the fulfilment of our promise.

### Sect 31: Valentinians

Against the Valentinians, who are also Gnostics, the eleventh, but the thirty-first in the series.

**31,1,1** After these so-called Ebionites, having gone through [their] wickedness, and having promised by the power of God [...] the others in order — which have the forms of other wild beasts and the venoms of reptiles, their bites and poisons, as much as can be seen in their teachings as from a gaping maw, conceived of a fire-darting or dreadful serpent and basilisk — I proceed to the heresy of the Valentinians, making my refutation of them as best I can; they have indeed given themselves the name of Gnostics too, and their varieties are ten in a single stroke

**31,1,2** of their dream-mongering about pairs and ogdoads and male-female aeons, struck in equal measure one [against] another; and I no longer arrange my composition by the times of succession, but proceed by advance from one to another.

**31,1,3** For all these sprang up at one time like fungi from the earth, and like noxious and foul-smelling shoots of weeds and thorny grass, and like a den of many scorpions all sprang into life from one time, and, as I said before, like misshapen fungi they have appeared at a single prompting, as has already been said about them by the most holy Irenaeus.

**31,1,4** For all these were contemporaries, each borrowing his evil pretext from another, one wanting [something] still greater than another [...]; for the sake of display he sketched out for himself in advance a variety of evil invention.

**31,1,5** And they all gave themselves the name of Gnostics — Valentinus, I mean, and the Gnostics who preceded him, but also Basilides, Satornilus, Colorbasus, Ptolemy, Secundus, Carpocrates and a good many others.

**31,1,6a** But though we have named them all here, because of the connection of all of them in one period of their emergence and evil growth [...], nevertheless we shall describe the malice of each one’s sowing separately.

**31,1,6b** For the present I shall turn to the heresiarch and tragedy-maker now before us — Valentinus, I mean — and his teaching, which derives from this grand ‘Gnostic’ profession, but [...] of condemnable nonsense, as it is found by the intelligent to be condemned and ridiculous.

**31,2,1** This Valentinus, then, follows in time those set before him, Basilides and Satornilus, Ebion and Cerinthus and Merinthus and those around them; for all these sprang up in life, to evil effect, at one time, though Cerinthus and Merinthus and Ebion and their followers rather a little earlier; for they sprang up together with those I have already set out before them.

**31,2,2a** Most people do not know his native land, or where he was born; for it has not readily been the care of any of the writers to point out this place.
