**31,28,6** And taking each one of the things said away from the truth [and] misusing the names, they have transferred them to their own system, so that, according to them, in all these words John does not mention the Lord Christ Jesus.

**31,28,7** For if he has named Father and Grace and Only-begotten and Truth and Word and Life and Man and Church, according to their system he has spoken of the first Ogdoad, in which there was as yet no Jesus, as yet no Christ, the teacher of John.

**31,28,8** But that the apostle spoke not of their pairings but about our Lord Jesus Christ, whom he knows to be the Word of God as well, he himself has made plain.

**31,28,9a** For summing up concerning the Word of whom he spoke above, in the beginning, he explains further, “And the Word became flesh and made his dwelling among us.”

**31,28,9b** But according to their system it was not the Word who became flesh, since he never even came outside the Pleroma, but the Saviour, who came into being from them all and is later in origin than the Word.

**31,29,1a** Learn, then, you senseless people, that Jesus who suffered for us, who dwelt among us, this very one is the Word of God.

**31,29,1b** For if some other of the Aeons had become flesh for our salvation, it would be reasonable that the apostle spoke about someone else; but if the Father’s Word who came down is himself also the one who went up, the only God’s only-begotten Son, made flesh on behalf of men according to the Father’s good pleasure, [John] has not composed his account about anyone else nor about an Ogdoad, but about the Lord Jesus Christ.

**31,29,2a** For according to them not even the Word became flesh in the principal sense.

**31,29,2b** For they say that the Saviour put on a psychic body, prepared from the dispensation by an ineffable providence so as to become visible and tangible.

**31,29,3** But flesh is the ancient formation made by God out of the dust, after the manner of Adam, which John declared that the Word of God had truly become.

**31,29,4a** And their first and primal Ogdoad is undone.

**31,29,4b** For since the Word and Only-begotten and Life and Light and Saviour and Christ and Son of God is proved to be one and the same, and this very one was made flesh for us, the tent-structure of [their] Ogdoad is undone.

**31,29,4c** And with this undone, their whole system has collapsed, the system that they falsely dream up as they run down the Scriptures, having fabricated a system of their own.

**31,29,5** Next, gathering expressions and names that lie scattered here and there, they transfer them, as we have said before, from what accords with nature to what is against nature, doing the like of those who propose to themselves whatever themes occur to them and then try to work them out from the poems of Homer,

**31,29,6** so that the less experienced think that Homer composed the verses on that theme worked up on the spur of the moment, and many are carried away by the pieced-together sequence of the verses, wondering whether Homer may not after all have composed them in just this way;

**31,29,7** as does the one who writes, by means of Homeric lines, of Heracles being sent by Eurystheus after the dog in Hades, as follows (for nothing prevents our mentioning these too, for the sake of example, since the attempt is similar and the same in both cases):

**31,29,8a** “So saying, he sent forth from his halls, groaning heavily, the hero Heracles, versed in mighty deeds, Eurystheus, son of Sthenelus, the son of Perseus, to bring out of Erebus the dog of hateful Hades.”

**31,29,8b** “And he went on his way like a mountain-bred lion trusting in his might, swiftly through the city, and all his friends followed together, young women and unwed youths and much-enduring old men, lamenting piteously, as for one going to his death.”

**31,29,8c** “And Hermes sent him forth, and grey-eyed Athena; for she knew in her heart how her brother laboured.”

**31,29,9a** Who among the guileless would not be carried away by these verses and suppose that Homer composed them thus on this theme?

**31,29,9b** But one who is experienced in the Homeric subject matter [will recognise the verses, but the theme he will not] recognise,

**31,29,10** knowing that one part of them was said of Odysseus, another of Heracles himself, another of Priam, another of Menelaus and Agamemnon; and taking them up and restoring each one to its own [book], he will do away with the [present] theme.

**31,29,11** So too the one who holds unswerving within himself the rule of truth, which he received through baptism, will recognise the names and the expressions and the parables taken from the Scriptures, but this blasphemous system he will not recognise.

**31,29,12** For even if he recognises the little stones, yet he will not accept the fox in place of the royal image; but restoring each one of the things said to its own order and fitting it to the body of the truth, he will lay their fiction bare and show it to be without substance.

**31,30,1** [But] since this stage-play still lacks its redemption, so that one may carry their mime through to its end and then bring the argument that demolishes it, we thought it well to show first the points in which the very fathers of this fable differ from one another, as being from different spirits of error.

**31,30,2** For from this too it will be possible to see most precisely (even before the demonstration) the firmness of the truth proclaimed by the church and the falsehood counterfeited by these men.

**31,30,3** For the church, although scattered throughout the whole inhabited world to the ends of the earth, yet having taken over from the apostles and from their disciples the faith in one God, Father Almighty, who made the heaven and the earth and the seas and all that is in them,

**31,30,4** and in one Christ Jesus, God’s Son, who took flesh for the sake of our salvation,

**31,30,5** and in the Holy Spirit, who by means of the prophets proclaimed the dispensations and the coming and the birth from the virgin and the passion and the rising from the dead and the ascension in the flesh into the heavens of the beloved [...] Christ Jesus, our Lord, and his advent from the heavens in the glory of the Father to sum up all things and to raise up all flesh of all humankind,

**31,30,6** so that to Christ Jesus our Lord and God and Saviour and King, according to the good pleasure of the invisible Father, every knee should bow, of things in heaven and on earth and under the earth, and every tongue should confess him, and that he should execute just judgement among all,

**31,30,7** sending the spiritual forces of wickedness and the angels who transgressed and fell into apostasy, and the ungodly and unrighteous and lawless and blasphemous among men, into the eternal fire,

**31,30,8** but to the righteous and holy, and to those who have kept his commandments and remained in his love (some from the beginning, others from repentance), granting life, he should bestow incorruption and secure for them eternal glory —

**31,31,1** having received this preaching and this faith, as we said before, the church, although scattered in the whole world, carefully guards it as if dwelling in one house, and believes these things alike as having one soul and the same heart, and in harmony proclaims and teaches and hands them on as possessing one mouth.

**31,31,2** For though the languages throughout the world are unlike, yet the tradition’s force is one and the same, and neither have the churches founded in the Germanies believed otherwise or handed down otherwise, nor those in the Iberias, nor those among the Celts, nor in the east, nor in Egypt, nor in Libya, nor those founded in the middle of the world;

**31,31,3** but just as the sun, God’s creature, is one and the same in the whole world, so too the [...] light, the preaching of the truth, shines everywhere and enlightens all men who wish to come to the knowledge of truth,

**31,31,4** and neither will one of those who preside in the churches who is very powerful in speech say anything other than these things (for no one is above his teacher), nor will one who is weak in speech diminish the tradition; for since the faith is one and the same, neither did one who is able to say much about it have anything over, nor did one who can say little have any lack.

**31,31,5** But that some know more or less according to their understanding does not come about by changing the subject matter itself and devising another God besides the Creator and Maker and Nourisher of this universe, as though they were not content with him, or another Christ or another Only-begotten,

**31,31,6** but by working out further all that has been said in parables and fitting it to the subject matter of the faith, and by recounting the activity and the dispensation of God that took place for humankind, and by making clear that God was long-suffering both over the apostasy of the angels who transgressed and over the disobedience of men;

**31,31,7** and by proclaiming why it is one and the same God who has made some things temporal and others eternal, and some heavenly and others earthly; and by understanding why God, though invisible, showed himself to the prophets, not in a single form but variously to various ones; and by pointing out why several covenants have been made with humankind, and by teaching what the character of each of the covenants is;

**31,31,8** and by searching out why God shut up all things in disobedience, that he might have mercy on all; and by giving thanks for the reason why the Word of God became flesh and suffered; and by proclaiming why the coming of the Son of God was at the last of the times, that is, why the beginning appeared at the end;

**31,31,9** and by unfolding all that lies in the Scriptures concerning the end and the things to come; and by not keeping silent as to why God has made the nations that had been despaired of co-heirs, fellow members of one body, and sharers with the saints;

**31,31,10** and by declaring how this mortal flesh shall be clothed in immortality and the corruptible in incorruption; and by proclaiming how he will say, “The one that was not a people, a people, and she who was not beloved, beloved”, and how “the children of the desolate are many more than those of her who has the husband” —

**31,31,11** for it was at these things and things like them that the apostle cried out, “O the depth of God’s riches and wisdom and knowledge, how inscrutable are his judgements and how untraceable his ways”; but it does not come about by devising, above the Creator and Fashioner, a Mother of him and of them, the Intention of an Aeon gone astray, and by coming to such a pitch of blasphemy;

**31,31,12** nor [by] falsely asserting that above her, again, there is a Pleroma, at one time of thirty Aeons, but now an innumerable tribe of them, as these teachers, truly destitute of divine understanding, say, while the whole church that exists has one and the same faith throughout the whole world, as we said before.

**31,32,1** Let us now see the unstable opinion of these men too: how, though they are perhaps two or three, they do not say the same things about the same matters, but declare things contrary both in substance and in names.

**31,32,2** For the first of them, Valentinus, who adapted the principles of the so-called Gnostic sect into the particular character of a school of his own, set them out as follows, defining that there is [a certain] unnameable dyad, of which one part is called Ineffable and the other Silence;

**31,32,3** then that from this dyad a second dyad was emitted, of which he calls one part Father and the other Truth; and that from this Tetrad there come forth as fruit Word and Life, Man and Church; and that this is the first Ogdoad.

**31,32,4** And from the Word and Life, he says, ten powers were emitted, as we have said before; and from Man and Church twelve, of which one revolted and fell short and brought about the rest of the business.

**31,32,5** And he posited two Limits: one lying between Depth and the remainder of the Pleroma, marking off the begotten Aeons from the unbegotten Father, and the other marking off their Mother from the Pleroma.

**31,32,6** And Christ too, he says, was emitted not from the Aeons within the Pleroma, but was brought forth with a kind of shadow by the Mother, when she had come to be outside, in accordance with her memory of the better things.

**31,32,7a** And this one, being male, cut off the shadow from himself and ran back up into the Pleroma, while the Mother, left behind with the shadow and emptied of the spiritual substance, brought forth a second son, and this one is the Demiurge, whom he also calls the Almighty of the things beneath him.

**31,32,7b** And he taught that a left-hand ruler was emitted along with him, in the same way as the falsely named Gnostics of whom we shall speak.

**31,32,8** And Jesus [too], he says, was emitted at one time from the one who was drawn back from their Mother [and] merged with the whole, that is, [from] Desired, at another from the one who ran back up into the Pleroma, that is, Christ, and at another from Man and Church.

**31,32,9a** And the Holy Spirit, he says, was emitted by Truth for the examination and the fruit-bearing of the Aeons, entering into them invisibly; through it the Aeons bear the plants of truth as fruit.

**31,32,9b** Here the words of Irenaeus against the Valentinians are complete.

**31,33,1** These things, then, and things of the kind [...], the aforementioned aged man Irenaeus, adorned in every respect by the Holy Spirit, put forward by [the] Lord as a noble athlete and anointed with the heavenly gifts that belong to the true faith and knowledge, having thrown and vanquished their whole nonsensical system, went through in detail, all the empty sounds that they utter.

**31,33,2** And in his following second book and in the others he refuted them most thoroughly and more abundantly, wishing, as it were, to drag away the one who had been thrown to the ground and put to rout, and to lead him in triumph before all and expose the shameless and feeble challenge of vain-mindedness in the very one who had been thrown.

**31,33,3** But we, content with the few things said by us and with what has been said and composed by these writers of the truth, and seeing that others have laboured — I mean Clement and Irenaeus and Hippolytus and many others, who have also made a marvellous refutation of them — did not wish to add much at all to the toil, as I said before, finding the aforementioned men sufficient and having in mind this very thing, that to every intelligent person their refutation will appear even from the very words in their teachings, against themselves.

**31,34,1** First, that their opinions differ among them, and one undertakes to demolish the other’s; second, that their fabulous inventions are incoherent, since no Scripture anywhere has said so, not the Mosaic law, not any prophet of those after Moses, nor indeed the Saviour nor his evangelists, nor yet the apostles.

**31,34,2** For were these things true, the Lord who came to give light to the world, and before him the prophets, and then the apostles too, who refuted idolatry and every lawless practice and did not shrink from writing against every lawless teaching and contrariness, would plainly have announced such things to us,

**31,34,3** especially since the Saviour himself says, “To those outside in parables, but to you the parables are to be spoken with their solution, of the kingdom of heaven.”

**31,34,4** At any rate, all the parables he spoke in the gospels he is seen to have explained at once; indeed, he tells what the grain of mustard is, and what the leaven, and who the woman who put the leaven into the three measures, what the vineyard, what the fig tree, what the sower, what the best ground;

**31,34,5** and in vain are these men possessed, driven by demons, of whom the most holy apostle Paul says, “In later times some will fall away from the teaching, paying attention to myths and teachings of demons.”

**31,34,6** And again holy James, saying of such teaching, “This wisdom is not one that comes down from above, but is earthly, natural, demonic; but the wisdom from above is first pure, then peaceable, compliant, impartial, filled with mercy and with good fruits”, and what follows — of which not a single fruit is found among those just mentioned.

**31,34,7** For among them is “disorder and every lawless deed”, both the offspring of demons and the hissings of serpents, each of them saying something different and in a different way at different times; among them no mercy, no compassion is found, but divisions and differences, and nowhere purity, nowhere peace, nowhere gentleness.

**31,35,1a** But again, compelled by the argument, although I promised to stop, I wish to recall a few of the things said among them and overthrow them.

**31,35,1b** For I care not for artful speech, but for the benefit of those who read.

**31,35,2** They say, then, that the twelfth Aeon, which came to be in deficiency, fell away entirely from the number of the twelve, and the twelfth number is lost.

**31,35,3** And they say that this came about through Judas, who was the twelfth, falling away, and so the twelfth number vanishing; and likewise also concerning the woman with the flow of blood and the woman who lost the one drachma out of the ten drachmas.

**31,35,4** But it is shown that neither will the twelfth Aeon be able to bear the part of Judas, as has been said before by the most holy Irenaeus — for Judas perished utterly, whereas the so-called twelfth Aeon, according to their fiction, was not emptied out; for the Conveyor, or the Limit-Setter, stood before it, saying “Iao” to it, as they themselves say, and so it was made firm —

**31,35,5a** nor will the woman who had a flow of blood for twelve years resemble their drama; for she was saved after twelve years, during which she was carried along by the blood of her flux.

**31,35,5b** For she did not continue free of the affliction for eleven years and then flow in the twelfth, but rather she was flowing during the eleven and in the twelfth was saved —

**31,35,6** nor did the woman who owned the ten drachmas lose the single one for ever, so that the Aeon of matter might be told in their fables as lost; for she lit the lamp and found the drachma.

**31,36,1** From these two or three arguments, then, all their dramatic inventions will at once be found by the sons of understanding and the children of the holy catholic church of God to be refuted, and feeble and stale.

**31,36,2** For, so as not to drive the treatise on to endless length by being carried along against the same people, having made my record up to the present account and putting an end to

**31,36,3** this great wickedness of theirs, I shall go on to those that follow, calling upon God as guide and helper of our weakness, so that we may be saved from this sect and from the sects mentioned before and from those that are yet to be brought to light, both for the lovers of learning and for those who wish to know with precision the empty babblings and incoherent bonds that are in the world.

**31,36,4a** For this man, having sown his own dreaming broadcast among many and having called himself a Gnostic, bound together many scorpions, so to speak, into one chain, as the old and vivid parable has it.

**31,36,4b** Namely, that scorpions, fastening themselves into a sort of chain one to another, up to ten or more, let themselves down from a ceiling or a roof and in this way craftily work harm on people.

**31,36,5** So too this man and those called Gnostics after him have become leaders of error, and each, having received the pretexts from him and having been taught by another, after his teacher made an addition to the error and introduced another sect, closely attached to the earlier one.

**31,36,6** And so by succession those very ones who were called Gnostics were divided into different sects; yet they had received their pretexts from Valentinus, as I said, and from those before him.

**31,36,7** Nevertheless, let the sects mentioned before, together with this sect of Valentinus, trampled by us with the teaching of the truth, be passed over, and let us examine those that follow in the power of God.

### Sect 32: Secundians

Against the Secundians, with whom Epiphanes and Isidore were joined, the twelfth, but the thirty-second in the series.

**32,1,1** Having passed through the sect of Valentinus, and having laboured much in his thorny sowing, with much, so to speak, [...] toil and a farmer’s hardship, we shall come to the remnants of his thorny sowing and of his reptilian beast-shape,

**32,1,2** having asked the Holy Spirit from the Lord, so as through him to shelter souls with the teaching that is in God and with reverent speech, and to be able to draw out the venoms from those who have thus been seized beforehand.

**32,1,3** And I shall begin to speak of these in order of succession, who became the successor of whom among those who took their start from him and taught another sowing besides his.

**32,1,4** Secundus, then, being one of them and wishing to think something beyond the rest, expounds everything in accordance with Valentinus, but made a louder noise ring out in the ears of the thunderstruck.

**32,1,5** For this man, being in accord with Valentinus, as I said before, but thinking beyond Valentinus, says that the first Ogdoad is a right-hand Tetrad and a left-hand Tetrad, teaching that the one is to be called light and the other darkness;

**32,1,6** and that the power that revolted and fell short is not from the thirty Aeons but after the thirty Aeons, so as to be from [their fruits, those that] came into being lower down after the other Ogdoad.

**32,1,7** And in all matters concerning Christ and the other doctrines he teaches in the same way as Valentinus, his own purveyor of the venom and giver of the poison.

**32,1,8** Since the strange novelties he propounds by way of difference are not many, I thought it right to be content with what has been said before, since they carry their refutation in themselves as well; nevertheless I shall say a few things about him too, so that I may not seem to have passed over his teachings for want of anything to say.

**32,1,9** For if right-hand and left-hand Tetrads are ranked among them, something will be found to be sought in the middle between the right and the left.

**32,1,10** For anything whatever that has right-hand and left-hand ranks stands in the middle of its left and right, and there can nowhere be right or left unless the distinction of each side should come about through a body that stands between.

**32,1,11** So then, O senseless Secundus and those deceived by you, the very middle, from which both left and right fall below it, will be some one thing,

**32,1,12** and what falls below it on the right and on the left cannot be alien to that one thing, and of necessity the whole will be brought back to the One that is, than which there is nothing higher, and nothing lesser or lowest except the things created by it.

**32,1,13a** And this will be plain to those who learn the truth, that God is one, Father and Son and Holy Spirit.

**32,1,13b** But if God is one, from whom are all things, there is neither anything left-hand in him nor any other deficiency nor anything inferior, except the things created by him.

**32,1,13c** And all things after Father and Son and Holy Spirit have been created well and have come forth into existence through him bountifully.

**32,2,1** But if, again, such a one, another serpent come forth against us, says that the things on the right and on the left exist outside the One, while he himself is in the very middle and draws the right to himself and takes delight in them, and calls these right and light, but thrusts away the left as alien to him and lying on the left, let him tell me where he gets this geometry, so as to arrange rightly an unchanging right and left.

**32,2,2** For with us right or left is fittingly named from the members fixed in the body, among which no interchange ever takes place.

**32,2,3a** But things outside us can be at one time right and at another left.

**32,2,3b** For to everyone looking towards the east the south, that is, the midday quarter, will be called right.

**32,2,3c** And the northern quarter, that is, the quarter of the Bear, left.

**32,2,4** But conversely, when a man has turned round towards the western parts, the quarters will be found to have the opposite names: what a moment before was right, southern and of the midday, now changes over to left, and the quarter that lay to the man’s left, whether the quarter of the Bear or the northern, changes over to his right.

**32,2,5a** Where, then, did the deceiver discover for himself his geometry concerning God?

**32,2,5b** And oh, for such abundant nonsense, which throws everything into confusion!

**32,2,6a** And he asserts that the Deficiency arose after the thirty Aeons.

**32,2,6b** From where, then, tell me, have you grasped the nature of the Deficiency, my good man, or of the power that revolted?

**32,2,7** For if you found that it is from a shoot of those above [...], not created but begotten (since for you and your master created things are not defined as created, but as begotten and as by participation, each nature receiving from the other, so that the things begotten in turn [...] spring up), then surely by your own argument you are arming yourself against yourself.

**32,2,8** For if both the lower power and the revolt have come into being from those above, and have grown, so to speak, and sprouted, then it shares in the gifts above, since the lower has fellowship with the Pleroma, and the Pleroma in turn with the lower, and this will in no way differ from that, nor that from this, since each communicates with the other at their boundaries.

**32,2,9** And by every argument you will be refuted as having the pasture of your error from the sowing of a demon, most pitiable of all the pitiable.

**32,3,1** But so that we may not pass over any of the things done and said among certain people, even if in each there should be many ringleaders who boast of inventing fables, one thing in place of another, beyond their own teachers, I shall go on describing those who are in this very sect but say things different from these men,

**32,3,2** I mean concerning Isidore’s Epiphanes, who on the pretext of exhortatory sayings brought himself down into a still deeper abyss of misery, having, to tell the truth, received his pretexts from Carpocrates, his own father according to the flesh, but having joined himself to the sect of the aforementioned Secundus and being himself one of the Secundians.

**32,3,3** For there is great difference between each of those who have gone astray and the next, and, so to speak, a rubbishy medley of empty babble.

**32,3,4** For this Epiphanes, as I said, being the son of Carpocrates, and of a mother called Alexandria, is, as I said before, connected with these men; on his father’s side he was of Cephallenian descent, [and] having lived seventeen years he ended his life rather quickly, the Lord ridding the world of the wicked as of thorns and providing something better for the inhabited world.

**32,3,5** And after his death those who had been led astray by him did not keep away from the plague that came from him.

**32,3,6** For in Samos he is honoured as a god even to this day; for the inhabitants founded a precinct for him and perform sacrifices and rites at each new moon, and they set up altars to him and erected a famous museum in his name, the so-called Museum of Epiphanes.

**32,3,7** For the Cephallenians, having been driven into such great error, sacrifice to him and pour libations, and feast in the precinct founded for him, and sing hymns to him.

**32,3,8** And because of the surpassing extent of his education, both general and Platonic, the whole deception of the aforementioned came about from him, both as regards the sect and as regards that other error — I speak of the one which drove the local people into a madness for idols.

**32,3,9a** This Epiphanes, at any rate, was connected with Secundus and those around him.

**32,3,9b** For he took the impress of his venomous outpouring, I mean the superfluous talk of an injurious, reptilian corruption.

**32,4,1a** And they say that Isidore, in his Exhortations, became the cause of his wickedness.

**32,4,1b** But whether he too happened to hold the same views and to take his start from them, or whether, having learned from philosophers, he was himself also a writer of exhortations, we have not been able to learn at all clearly about Isidore.

**32,4,1c** But all these men cobble together the same business.

**32,4,2** First, then, Epiphanes himself, together with his father and the patron of the sect, Carpocrates, and those around him, laid down the law that men’s wives should be held in common, taking his pretext from Plato’s Republic and fulfilling his own lust.

**32,4,3** And he makes his start from this, saying that it is recorded in the gospel that the Saviour says there are three eunuchs: the one made a eunuch by men, the one from birth, and the one who has willingly made himself a eunuch for the sake of the kingdom of heaven; and he says:

**32,4,4a** “Some, then, become eunuchs by necessity, not by reason.”

**32,4,4b** “But those who have made themselves eunuchs for the sake of the kingdom of heaven, because of the things that result from marriage, they say, [adopt this] reasoning, fearing the distraction [over the provision] of necessities.”

**32,4,5a** “[And by] ‘Better to marry than to be burned’ he says the apostle means: ‘Do not cast your soul into the fire, holding on day and night and fearing that you may fall away from continence.’”

**32,4,5b** “For a soul engaged in holding on is cut off from hope.”

**32,4,6a** “Hold on, then,” — as I said before, bringing the substance of his exhortation into the open, Isidore says word for word in his Ethics — “to a quarrelsome woman, so that you may not be torn away from the grace of God, and having discharged the fire as seed, pray with a good conscience.”

**32,4,6b** “But when,” he says, “your thanksgiving sinks into petition, and you come [to the point] of falling from then on, not having succeeded, marry.”

**32,4,7** Then again he says, “But someone is young or poor or prone to fall (that is, weak), and does not wish to marry in accordance with the word; let this man not separate himself from the brother.”

**32,4,8a** And the wretch, procuring for himself certain shameful suspicions, puts on a drama: “Let him say,” [for] he says, “‘I have entered into the sanctuary, I can suffer nothing.’”

**32,4,8b** “But if he has a suspicion, let him say, ‘Brother, lay your hand on me, so that I may not sin,’ and he will receive help both of the mind and of the senses.”

**32,4,8c** “Let him only wish to accomplish what is good, and he will attain it.”

**32,4,9a** Then again he says, “Sometimes we say with the mouth, ‘We do not wish to sin,’ but the mind is bent on sinning.”

**32,4,9b** “Such a one does not do what he wishes because of fear, lest the punishment be reckoned to him.”

**32,4,9c** “But humankind has some things that are necessary and natural, [and some] that are natural only; it has clothing, which is necessary and natural, and natural too is the matter of sexual pleasure, but [not] necessary”;

**32,5,1** “these sayings I have set out,” [says] the one who wrote against them, “to refute those who do not live rightly,” both Basilideans and Carpocratians and those from Valentinus and those named after Epiphanes, with whom indeed Secundus, treated above, was joined.

**32,5,2a** For each derives from the other, either this one imparting to that one or that one to this.

**32,5,2b** Having trafficked in dreadful things, and though differing from one another in some point, they nevertheless placed themselves in one sect,

**32,5,3** “so that they even taught that they have licence to sin because of their perfection, or that they will in any case be saved by nature, even if they sin now, because of their innate election, whereas the forefathers of these doctrines do not even allow them to do the same things.”

**32,5,4a** “And these too, as reaching out to what is loftier and more Gnostic, speak of the first Tetrad thus: ‘There is a certain Pre-Beginning before all things, pre-inconceivable, both ineffable and unnameable, which I call Singleness.’”

**32,5,4b** “With this Singleness coexists a power, which I also name Oneness.”

**32,5,5** “This Oneness and Singleness, being the One, sent forth, without sending forth, a beginning over all things, intelligible, unbegotten and invisible, which beginning the account calls Monad.”

**32,5,6a** “With this Monad coexists a power of the same substance with it, which I also name the One.”

**32,5,6b** “These powers, Singleness and Oneness, Monad and the One, sent forth the remaining emissions of the Aeons.”

**32,6,1** Then those who wrote the truth well [about] these men refuted [them] in their own writings, both Clement (whom some call an Alexandrian, others an Athenian),

**32,6,2** and also the holy Irenaeus, who, ridiculing them, came out bringing forward that tragic cry over what was said before, [this]: “Alas, alas, and woe, woe.”

**32,6,3** “Truly one may utter the tragic cry over such a calamity of those who have written these ridiculous things, over such coining of names and such great audacity, in that he has shamelessly set names to his lie.”

**32,6,4** “For in saying, ‘There is a certain Pre-Beginning before all things, pre-inconceivable, which I call Singleness,’ and again, ‘With this Singleness coexists a power, which I also name Oneness,’ he has most plainly confessed both that what he says is his own fiction and that he himself has set names to the fiction, names set by no one else before.”

**32,6,5** “And clearly it is he himself who has ventured to coin these names, and had he himself not been present in life, the truth would have had no name.”

**32,6,6** “Nothing, then, prevents someone else too from defining names in this way on the same subject”;

**32,6,7** then, after this, the same blessed Irenaeus, as we said before, himself also uttered ridiculous words, jesting with a different set of names of his own as fitting counterparts to their nonsense, and fashioning kinds of melons and cucumbers and gourds as though they were certain underlying realities, as would be plain to the lovers of learning from what they have read before.

**32,7,1** Others of them, again, have called their first and primal Ogdoad by these names: first Pre-Beginning, then Inconceivable, the third Ineffable and the fourth Invisible.

**32,7,2** And they say that from the first, Pre-Beginning, there was emitted in the first and fifth [place] Beginning; from Inconceivable, in the second and the sixth place, Incomprehensible; from Ineffable, in the third and the seventh place, Unnameable; and from Invisible, Unbegotten, the Pleroma of the first Ogdoad.

**32,7,3** These powers they want to pre-exist Depth and Silence, so that they may be seen to be more perfect than those who are perfect and more Gnostic than the Gnostics; against whom one might justly cry out: O babbling sophists!

**32,7,4a** For even about the Depth itself there are different opinions among them.

**32,7,4b** For some say that he is without consort, neither male nor female nor anything at all, while others say that he is male-female, attaching to him the nature of a hermaphrodite.

**32,7,5** And others, again, fasten Silence on him as his bedfellow, so that there may come to be a first pairing, and so that from him and from her they may thus stage the rest.

**32,7,6** And there is among them a great deal of babbling dreaming that carries their mind down into deep sleep.

**32,7,7** And why should I wear myself out at length, when their system, and the refutation and overthrow of them, are plain to detect from what has been said before, for anyone who wishes to hold fast to his own life and not be deceived by empty fables?

**32,7,8a** But let this be said by me about these men.

**32,7,8b** And passing this sect by, [I shall go on] to those that follow, looking out for myself a safe road and a smooth path, so that, going through and refuting the wickednesses of these, I may in the power of God keep safe both myself and those who hear, through the teaching of our Lord and true contemplation.

**32,7,9** Having trampled, [then], with the shoe of the gospel this viper, like the mouse-hunting snake, which resembles many other vipers, let us examine those that follow.

### Sect 33: Ptolemaeans

Against the Ptolemaeans, the thirteenth, but the thirty-third in the series.

**33,1,1** Secundus’ followers and the Epiphanes we have named, who bartered the pretext taken from Isidore for a notion of his own, are succeeded by Ptolemy, who belongs to that same sect of those called Gnostics and is [one] of the followers of Valentinus, along with certain others, but who propounds things different from his own teachers; and those who follow him boast of his name, being called Ptolemaeans.

**33,1,2** This Ptolemy, then, and those with him, has come forth to us still more expert than his own teachers, having devised excesses of a certain addition to their account.

**33,1,3** For he devised and bestowed two consorts on the god who among them is called Depth; and these he also called dispositions, Thought and Will.

**33,1,4a** And Thought, he says, always coexisted with him, ever thinking of emitting something, while Will arose in him afterwards.

**33,1,4b** For first he thought of emitting [something], then, he says, he willed it.

**33,1,5** Hence too, when these two dispositions, or powers (for he calls them powers too), Thought and Willing, had been as it were blended with each other, the emission of the Only-begotten and Truth took place as a pair.

**33,1,6** And these came forth as types and images of the two dispositions of the Father, visible images of the invisible: Mind of the Will, and Truth of Thought; and for this reason the male has become the image of the [supervening] Will, [and] the female of the unbegotten Thought.

**33,1,7a** The Will, then, became the power of Thought.

**33,1,7b** For Thought always thought of the emission, yet it was not able by itself to emit what it thought; but when the power of the Will came upon it, then it emitted what it thought.

**33,2,1** Alas for such nonsense of the vain-minded man; for this would not be accepted even in the case of a man by anyone of sound mind, much less in the case of God.

**33,2,2** And Homer seems to me more sensible than he, in that he describes Zeus as taking thought with himself, anxious and vexed and spending the whole night sleepless over how he might plot against the Achaeans, since Thetis had asked him to make the leaders of the Greeks, and the Greeks themselves, pay the penalty for the outrage against Achilles.

**33,2,3** For this Ptolemy has devised nothing more reasonable for the glory of the Father of all in his system, who is also called Depth, than what was said by Homer of Zeus, but rather he has conceived of Zeus himself,

**33,2,4** as one who took the idea from Homer; for [one] might fairly say [that he has held] the Homeric conception of Zeus and the Achaeans, having vomited out such great audacity of his own, rather than [the one] concerning the Master of all things, who at the very moment of thinking has also accomplished what he willed, and at the very moment of willing also thinks what he has willed, thinking when he wills and willing when he thinks, being wholly thought,

**33,2,5** wholly will, wholly mind, wholly light, wholly eye, wholly hearing, wholly the source of all good things, and held within no passion; for he is God, and not anxious and at a loss like the Depth, or rather Zeus, of whom Homer spoke, and Ptolemy imitated him in speaking of the Depth.

**33,2,6a** But for a fuller refutation of the deceiver I shall next set out, appended in order, the seductive and poisonous words written by Ptolemy himself to a certain woman, Flora (so that no one may think that we refute the deceiver from hearsay only, without having first read the teaching [falsely] composed by him; for besides what has been said, he is not ashamed to blaspheme the law of God given through Moses), and they are as follows:

**33,2,6b** Ptolemy to Flora.

**33,3,1** “That the law laid down through Moses, my fair sister Flora, has not been grasped by many, since they knew precisely neither the one who laid it down nor its commandments, will, I think, be easy for you too to see, once you have learned the conflicting opinions about it.”

**33,3,2** “For some say that it was laid down by God and the Father, while others, turning the opposite way to these, maintain that it was laid down by the adversary, the corrupting devil, just as they also attribute to him the making of the world, saying that he is the father and maker of this universe.”

**33,3,3** “[But] these have [utterly] stumbled, disagreeing with one another, and each of them by their own fault missing the truth of the matter in hand.”

**33,3,4** “For neither does it appear to have been laid down by the perfect God and Father (for this follows), being incomplete and needing to be completed by another, and having commandments alien to the nature and mind of such a God; nor again

**33,3,5** to attach to the injustice of the adversary a law [...] that [does away with] doing injustice; [...] and of people who do not see anything of what follows, [in accordance with] the words spoken by the Saviour: for ‘a house or a city split against itself is unable to stand’, our Saviour declared.”

**33,3,6** “Further, the apostle says that the world’s making is [his] own, and that all things came into being through him and without him nothing came into being, having stripped away beforehand the baseless wisdom of the liars, and that it is not of a corrupting god, but of one who is just and hates evil; and [this] is the view of thoughtless men, who take no account of the providence of the craftsman and who are blinded in the eye not of the soul alone but of the body as well.”

**33,3,7a** “That these, then, have missed the truth is plain to you from what has been said.”

**33,3,7b** “And each of them has suffered this in their own way, the one party through not knowing the God of righteousness, the other through not knowing the Father of all, whom the only one who knew him alone revealed when he came.”

**33,3,8** “It remains for us, who have been deemed worthy of the [knowledge] of both of these, to make plain to you and to set out precisely both the law itself, of what sort it is, and the one by whom it was laid down, the lawgiver, presenting proofs [of what] we shall say from the words of our Saviour, through which alone it is possible to be led without stumbling to the comprehension of the things that are.”

**33,4,1a** “First, then, it must be learned that that whole law contained in the Pentateuch of Moses was not laid down by some one person, I mean not by God alone, but there are some of its commandments that were laid down by men as well.”

**33,4,1b** “And the words of the Saviour teach us that it is divided into three.”

**33,4,2** “For it is divided between God himself and his legislation, and [it is divided] also between Moses (not in the sense that God legislates through him, but in that Moses too, starting from his own thought, legislated certain things), and it is divided between the elders of the people, [who] themselves too are found to have inserted certain commandments of their own.”

**33,4,3** “How this is so is shown from the words of the Saviour, as you may now learn.”

**33,4,4a** “Conversing somewhere with those who were debating with him over divorce — the divorce which the law had made permissible — the Saviour said to them, ‘Moses, because of your hardness of heart, permitted a man to put away his wife.’”

**33,4,4b** “‘For from the beginning it has not been so.’”

**33,4,4c** “‘For God,’ he says, ‘joined this union together, and what the Lord joined together, let man,’ he said, ‘not separate.’”

**33,4,5** “Here he shows that the law of God is one thing, the law that forbids a woman to be separated from her husband, and that of Moses another, the law that because of hardness of heart permits this pair to be separated.”

**33,4,6a** “And indeed in this Moses legislates things contrary to God; for it is contrary to not [...] separating.”

**33,4,6b** “If, however, we examine also the mind of Moses, according to which he legislated this, it will be found that he did this not by his own choice, but by necessity, because of the weakness of those to whom the law was given.”

**33,4,7** “For since these could not keep the mind of God, in that they were not permitted to put away their wives, with whom some of them lived unpleasantly and were in danger of being turned aside by this further into injustice and from injustice into destruction, Moses, wishing to cut out this unpleasantness of theirs,

**33,4,8** through which they were also in danger of perishing, legislated for them of himself a second law, the law of divorce, as one exchanging, in the circumstances, a lesser evil for a greater, so that, if they could not keep that one,

**33,4,9** they might at least keep this one and not be turned aside into injustices and wickednesses, through which a most complete destruction was going to follow upon them.”

**33,4,10** “This is his mind, according to which he is found legislating in opposition to God; but that the law of Moses himself is shown here to be different from the law of God is beyond dispute, even if for now we have shown it through one instance only.”

**33,4,11a** “And that there are also certain traditions of the elders interwoven in the law, the Saviour makes plain as well.”

**33,4,11b** “‘For God,’ he says, ‘said: Honour your father and your mother, that it may be well with you.’”

**33,4,12** “‘But you,’ he says, addressing the elders, ‘have said: Whatever benefit you might have had from me is a gift to God; and you have annulled the law of God for the sake of the tradition of you elders.’”

**33,4,13a** “‘And Isaiah proclaimed this, saying: This people pays me honour with the lips, yet their heart stays far away from me.’”

**33,4,13b** “‘And in vain do they worship me, teaching as teachings the commandments of men.’”

**33,4,14a** “Clearly, then, from these words that whole law is shown to be divided into three; for we have found in it the legislation of Moses himself and of the elders and of God himself.”

**33,4,14b** “This division of that whole law, then, divided by us in this way, has brought to light what is true in it.”

**33,5,1** “But again, the one part, the law of God himself, is divided into three: into the pure legislation unmixed with evil, which is also properly called law, which the Saviour came not to abolish but to fulfil (for what he fulfilled was not alien to him, [but it needed fulfilment]; for it did not have perfection), and into the law interwoven with the worse and with injustice, which the Saviour did away with as being alien to his own nature;

**33,5,2** and it is further divided into the part that is typical and symbolic, legislated after the image of the spiritual and superior things, which the Saviour transferred from the perceptible and visible to the spiritual and invisible.”

**33,5,3** “Now the law of God that is pure and not intermixed with the worse is the Decalogue itself, those ten words divided between the two tablets, for the prohibition of what is to be avoided and the enjoining of what is to be done; and these, though they contained pure legislation, yet did not have perfection, and stood in need of fulfilment from the Saviour.”

**33,5,4** “And the law interwoven with injustice is the one laid down for the retaliation and repayment of those who have done wrong first, ordering that eye be struck out for eye and tooth for tooth, and that murder be avenged with murder; for the second who does wrong does wrong no less, differing only in the order and doing the same deed.”

**33,5,5** “And this commandment was and is just in another respect, laid down because of the weakness of those to whom the law was given, as a departure from the pure law; but it is alien to the nature and the goodness of the Father of the universe.”

**33,5,6** “And perhaps this was fitting, but rather it was forced by necessity; for he who did not wish even the one murder to take place, in saying ‘You shall not murder’, by commanding that the murderer be murdered in return, enacting a further law and acting as umpire over a double murder, he who had forbidden the one was outwitted by necessity without being aware of it.”

**33,5,7** “This is why the Son who came from him did away with this part of the law, though he confessed that this part too belonged to God, [just as] it is reckoned to the old school both in the other passages and where he said, ‘God said: Whoever reviles his father or his mother, let him come to his end by death.’”

**33,5,8** “And the typical part of it is the part laid down after the image of the spiritual and superior things, I mean what was legislated concerning offerings and circumcision and the sabbath and fasting and the Passover and the unleavened bread and the like.”

**33,5,9** “For all these, being images and symbols, were transferred when the truth was made manifest; as regards their outward appearance and [their] bodily performance they were abolished, but as regards their spiritual meaning they were taken up, the names staying unchanged while the realities were altered.”

**33,5,10** “For the Saviour commanded us to offer offerings, but not those made with irrational animals or with these incenses, but those made with spiritual praises and glorifications and thanksgiving, and with fellowship with our neighbours and kindness towards them.”

**33,5,11** “And he wants us to be circumcised with a circumcision, yet one not of the foreskin of the body but of the heart, the spiritual one.”

**33,5,12** “And to keep the sabbath [as well]; for he wants us to be idle from evil works.”

**33,5,13a** “And to fast as well; yet he does not want us to fast the bodily fast, but the spiritual one, in which there is abstinence from all things base.”

**33,5,13b** “Nevertheless, the fast according to outward appearance is kept among our people too, since it too can contribute something to the soul when it is done with reason, when it is done neither in imitation of certain people nor out of habit nor because of the day, as though a day had been appointed [for] it.”

**33,5,14** “And at the same time it serves also to call the true fast to mind, so that those still unable to keep that fast may have the remembrance of it from the fast according to outward appearance.”

**33,5,15** “And that the Passover likewise and the unleavened bread were images, Paul the apostle also makes plain, saying, ‘Christ our Passover has been sacrificed,’ and ‘that you may be,’ he says, ‘unleavened, not partaking of leaven (and by leaven he here means wickedness), but may be a new lump.’”

**33,6,1** “Thus, then, the very law that is acknowledged to be of God is divided into three: into the part fulfilled by the Saviour (for ‘You shall not murder, you shall not commit adultery, you shall not swear falsely’ is included in not being angry, not lusting and not swearing);

**33,6,2a** and it is divided also into the part that is completely abolished.”

**33,6,2b** “For ‘eye in return for eye and tooth in return for tooth’, interwoven with injustice and itself containing a work of injustice, was abolished by the Saviour through its opposites.”

**33,6,3** “And opposites abolish one another: ‘For I say to you, do not resist the evil one at all, but if anyone strikes you, turn to him the other cheek as well.’”

**33,6,4** “And it is divided also into the part that was transferred and changed from the bodily to the spiritual, this symbolic part that was legislated after the image of the superior things.”

**33,6,5** “For the images and the symbols, being representations of other things, were rightly in force so long as the truth was not present; but now that the truth is present, one must do the things of the truth, not those of the image.”

**33,6,6** “And his disciples and the apostle Paul showed these things: the part concerning the images, as we have already said, by showing it through the Passover and the unleavened bread for our sake; the part concerning the law interwoven with injustice, by saying that ‘the law of commandments in ordinances has been abolished’; and the part concerning the law unmixed with the worse, in saying, ‘The law indeed is holy, and the commandment is holy, just and good.’”

**33,7,1** “As far, then, as it is possible to speak briefly, I think it has been sufficiently shown to you both that a legislation of human origin slipped in, and that the very law of God is divided threefold.”

**33,7,2a** “What remains for us is who this god may be who laid down the law.”

**33,7,2b** “But this too, I think, has been shown to you in what was said before, if you have listened carefully.”

**33,7,3** “For if it was laid down neither by the perfect God himself, as we have taught, nor yet by the devil, which it is not even lawful to say, then the one who laid down the law is someone other than these.”

**33,7,4** “And this one is the craftsman and maker of this whole world and of the things in it; being different from the substances of these two, [and] standing in the middle between them, he may rightly bear the name of the Midst as well.”

**33,7,5** “And if the perfect God is good according to his own nature, as indeed he is (for our Saviour declared that one only is the good God, his own Father, whom he himself revealed), and if the one who is of the nature of the adversary is evil and wicked, marked by injustice, then the one who stands in the middle between these, being neither good nor yet evil nor unjust, might in a proper sense be termed just, as the umpire of the justice that is his.”

**33,7,6** “And this god will be inferior to the perfect God and less than his justice, inasmuch as he is begotten and not unbegotten (for the Father is the one unbegotten, from whom are all things, all things depending on him in their proper way), but he will be greater and more sovereign than the adversary, and will be of a different substance and nature from the substance of each of these two.”

**33,7,7** “For the substance of the adversary is corruption and darkness (for he is material and divided into many parts), while the unbegotten Father of all has for his substance incorruption and light itself, simple and uniform; and the substance of this one brought forth a certain twofold power, while he himself is an image of the better one.”

**33,7,8** “And do not let this trouble you for the present, as you wish to learn how from one first principle of all things, which is [simple] and is acknowledged and believed by us, unbegotten and incorruptible and good, these natures too came to exist, that of corruption and [that] of the Midst, which are of unlike substance, when the good by its nature begets and brings forth things like itself and of the same substance.”

**33,7,9** “For you will learn next, God granting, both their first principle and their begetting, when you are deemed worthy of the apostolic tradition, which we too have received by succession, together with the measuring of all words by the teaching of our Saviour.”

**33,7,10a** “These things said to you in few words, my sister Flora, I have not set down feebly; I wrote beforehand of their brevity, and at the same time I have set out the matter in hand sufficiently; and they will contribute the greatest benefits to you for what follows, if, like fair and good ground that has received fertile seeds, you bring forth the fruit that comes from them.”

**33,7,10b** Here ends Ptolemy to Flora.

**33,8,1** But who will endure these words and the madness of this charlatan and of those with him, I mean Ptolemy and those around him, who stir up and cobble together fictions to such a degree?

**33,8,2** For neither any of the ancient tragedians, nor those after them who were mimics in their manner, I mean Philistion and his like and Diogenes who wrote the Incredible Things, or all the others who have written down and recited fables, were able to fashion so great a falsehood,

**33,8,3** as these men, carried boldly against their own life, have contrived dreadful things for themselves and have entangled the mind of the people who believe them in foolish questions and endless genealogies,

**33,8,4** not knowing themselves the things that are in their hands, and yet professing to mark out the boundaries of the heavenly things with certain measures, and taking upon themselves a knowledge of certain midwife mothers as though they were heavenly, mothers that do not exist, as if they existed.

**33,8,5** And if someone who has heard these men is one of the most foolish, thinking because of the falsehood that he has learned something lofty from them, he will easily be led astray (for “every bird gathers with its kind, and a man will cling to his like,” says the Scripture);

**33,8,6a** but if anyone of the intelligent, who possess sound reasoning, should come upon them, he will laugh at such great nonsense, and from the very system of the things said among them he will perceive its refutation.

**33,8,6b** For these men are refuted in every way, arming against themselves the falsehoods of their own vain labour.

**33,8,7** For from where, you Ptolemaean men and Ptolemaean women, do you have the measures of the Depth, and the midwiferies of the births, and the knowledge of the generative systems?

**33,8,8** For as though you had been present and had beheld the natures of the heavenly things, and as though you had existed before the very one called Depth among you, you profess to show us the knowledge of it,

**33,8,9** although none of the prophets has at any time said this, neither Moses himself nor those before him nor those who came after him, neither the evangelists nor the apostles; unless, that is, you were to cite the pagan-fabled poems of Orpheus and Hesiod and Hicesius and Stesichorus, among whom the generations of men were transformed into the names of gods, and the deeds done among men were turned into poetic drama.

**33,8,10** For they too thought in this way, [and] having deified Zeus and Rhea and Hera and Athena and Apollo and Aphrodite, and having honoured their vices and their births, they cast the world into the illusion of polytheism and idolatry.

**33,8,11a** But I shall no longer have much need for the overthrow and refutation of you, Ptolemy, and of your followers, since your forefathers have already received their refutation sufficiently.

**33,8,11b** And having established through what has been said the disgrace laid against you, I shall go through the error of the others, calling upon God as helper of our modest ability, so that I may detect and lead in triumph the system wickedly devised by each group, asking for grace from God for the fulfilment of what my endeavour has promised.

**33,9,1** But so that your three little phrases, which you vaunted in writing to Flora, that little woman, O Ptolemy (for, in the apostle’s words, serpent-like teachings are forever leading astray little women heaped up with sins), may not go unexamined, I shall set out the very phrases here at the fitting point, and next, in due order, I shall also make their overthrow, which is necessary so that no root of your tare-like sowing may be left behind.

**33,9,2** For you assert, sir, that the law has been divided three ways, and that one part of it has something from God, another from Moses, and another from the elders.

**33,9,3a** And that you cannot demonstrate anywhere [the very thing] which you suppose to have been written by the elders, that is plain.

**33,9,3b** For nowhere in the law are the traditions of the elders contained.

**33,9,3c** But, knowing neither the books nor the truth, you indulge in fancies and slander, ignorant too of the coherent order of every exact knowledge.

**33,9,4a** For the traditions of the elders are called deuteroses among the Jews.

**33,9,4b** And these are four: one, that which is current under the name of Moses; a second, that of the one called Rabbi Akiba; a third, that of Adda, or Judah; a fourth, that of the sons of Hasmonaeus.

**33,9,5** Otherwise, where can you, contentious man, unsettled in your judgement, show, within the Pentateuch’s five books and God’s legislation, that the saying reported by the Saviour was spoken, “[Whoever] shall say to his father, ‘Korban’, that is, ‘a gift’, he shall receive no benefit from him at all”?

**33,9,6a** But you cannot show it.

**33,9,6b** So then your argument has collapsed, since the saying is nowhere contained in the Pentateuch.

**33,9,6c** And in vain have you beguiled with fancies Flora, whom you deceived.

**33,9,7a** But the things by Moses [...] were themselves also legislated not apart from God, but from God through Moses, as is shown also from the Saviour’s own verdict.

**33,9,7b** For the very testimonies you brought forward you have gathered against yourself.

**33,9,8a** For the Lord says in the gospel, “Moses wrote according to your hardness of heart.”

**33,9,8b** Yet Moses did not write what he wrote apart from the will of God; rather, he legislated from the Holy Spirit.

**33,9,9a** For in the gospel the Lord says, “What God has yoked together, let man not separate.”

**33,9,9b** And how he yoked them together he went through [...] the saying, so that we might learn, saying, “On this account a man will leave behind his father and his mother and be knit to his wife, and the two will be one flesh.”

**33,9,10** Then he adds, saying, “What therefore God has yoked together, let man not separate”; and yet the Lord nowhere said this when he formed Adam and Eve, except only, “Let us make for him a helper corresponding to him.”

**33,9,11** But this was spoken by Adam when he arose from sleep and said, “This now is bone out of my bones and flesh out of my flesh; this one shall be called woman, because this one was taken out of her man”; then he says, “On this account a man will leave behind his father and his mother and be knit to his wife, and the two will be one flesh.”

**33,9,12a** Since, then, it was not God who said this but Adam who said it, while the Lord bears witness in the gospel that the saying spoken through Adam is God’s, he has shown from the very subject that there Adam spoke but uttered it by the will of God, and here likewise Moses legislated, God having announced the legislation to him.

**33,9,12b** And so these two phrases of yours have already fallen, and not from far afield, but from the very citation itself.

**33,9,13a** And that it is God’s legislation is plain.

**33,9,13b** But everywhere God legislates some things for seasons, some for types, and some for the revelation of the good things that were to come, whose fulfilment our Lord Jesus Christ, when he came, showed in the gospel.

**33,10,1** But taking up again the matters of your other division, that of gods, once more divided three ways, I shall show that these too have been slanderously misrepresented by you, and are nothing other than the work of a charlatan.

**33,10,2** For what third god is presented to us, compounded of two likenesses and being neither of the two, having neither the wickedness and injustice, as you said, nor the goodness and luminous essence, but being, as the midmost, just?

**33,10,3a** For indeed, being truly a stranger to all justice, you are naturally ignorant of what justice ever is, thinking it something other than goodness.

**33,10,3b** But you will be thoroughly refuted, you knave and stranger to the truth; for what is just comes into being from no other source than goodness, nor will anyone become good in any other way unless he is just.

**33,10,4a** Hence also the Lord, praising the legislation and its just men, said, “You adorn the prophets’ graves and build memorials of the just, and your fathers put them to death.”

**33,10,4b** And from where did prophets and just men come, if not from the goodness of the Father?

**33,10,5** And to show that the just man stands within the portion of goodness, he said, “Become like your Father who is in the heavens, for he makes his sun rise upon good and wicked and sends rain upon just and unjust”, to show that the just is good and the good just, and the wicked unjust and the unjust wicked.

**33,10,6a** But the interweaving of the law that you spoke of, you cannot demonstrate.

**33,10,6b** For you have been caught slandering the law, fastening a certain interweaving upon it, on the ground that the law has said, “An eye in return for an eye and a tooth in return for a tooth”, and since the murderer is put to death by the law.

**33,10,7** But from the very dealings of our Lord Jesus Christ it will be shown that it was not some interweaving, but that it was the same legislation and has the same effect as the commandment spoken by the Saviour, “If anyone strikes you upon the right jaw, turn the other to him as well.”

**33,10,8a** And [indeed] the law [too] long ago was establishing this, saying, “eye for eye”, that is, “turn your cheek to him.”

**33,10,8b** For in order to escape what he would suffer if he struck, a man would offer his cheek to the one striking him, knowing that, if he took out an eye, he would undergo the same treatment on account of the law.

**33,11,1** For as a father wishes to train his children and, adapting himself to each age, advances in his training, and does not train the little infant in the same way as the lad, nor the lad in the same way as the youth, nor the young man in the same way as the full-grown man,

**33,11,2** but for the suckling discipline comes by a finger, for a bigger child by a slap of the hand, for a lad by a strap, for a youth by a rod, and for a man there is punishment of the greater offences by the sword through law, so the Lord too legislated in succession what suited each generation.

**33,11,3** For those he schooled in self-control by fear, as though speaking to small children unacquainted with the power of the Holy Spirit, while the perfect he deemed worthy of perfect mysteries.

**33,11,4** For in the gospel too he speaks somewhat thus to the disciples in many places, “You do not know what I am doing, but afterwards you will know”, that is, when you have become perfect; and again, “They did not know, until he rose from the dead.”

**33,11,5** And Paul: “For you were not yet able, nor are you able even now”, showing that as the seasons advance the commandments are more perfect, being indeed the same, but changed into another form, and shaped in one way for the young, and in another for the more mature.

**33,11,6** For when the law legislated “eye for eye”, it did not say to them, Put out an eye for an eye, but, If anyone puts out an eye, the eye of him that did so shall be put out; and each man, sparing his own body, would give up his cheek to be struck and would not strike.

**33,11,7** And from that time the things now clearly spoken in the gospel were being observed: then by necessity, as by young people under discipline, but now by choice, as by perfect people who obey.

**33,11,8** But if you assert that this is an entanglement, the saying “eye for eye” and the putting of the murderer to death, see, about the day of judgement too we see the Saviour saying, “that his master will come” (and this he said about himself, since he is master of all), and he says, “and he will cut that servant in two and will put his portion with the unbelievers.”

**33,11,9** So then you are arming yourself against the Saviour too, again slandering his words, and you might claim that he is not good (although begotten of a good Father and being good), but just, and other than the Father.

**33,11,10** Then you can estrange even him from the Father’s essence, sir, you who have again come forward before us as a divider and surveyor of the laws, dividing everything three ways.

**33,11,11** And in saying that some things have been written in the law symbolically, as types, you have touched on a little of the truth, so that by means of the little you might be able to deceive in the rest as well.

**33,11,12** For in reality “these things happened to them by way of type, and were written down for our admonition, upon whom the ends of the ages have arrived”, [as] the most holy apostle declares when he speaks of circumcision and the sabbath and the other matters.

**33,11,13** Would that you had spoken the truth in everything and were no longer bringing us a third, intermediate god who does not exist, or rather bringing him to yourself and to those deceived by you.

**33,12,1** I think that in the present instance too enough has been said against your phrases, you charlatan; having made the refutation of them, I shall go on to the heresies that follow, calling as usual upon the same God to aid our modest powers in tracking down the refutation of each counterfeit heresy.

**33,12,2** For that with his own hissings, like a shark come up from the sea and calling forth a viper, he deceived Flora by his letter, both her and others along with her, has been shown in what has been said; but in the

**33,12,3** net of the truth, whose figure the Lord revealed in the gospel as the kingdom of the heavens, we have wrapped him up and, having proved him to be one of the bad fish by making his wicked words detectable, we have defeated him by the teaching of the true faith.

**33,12,4** Having crushed him, then, in the power of God, let us for our part give thanks to God, and let us set about proceeding in like manner, as I said before, to those that follow.

## Anacephalaeosis III

The following are contained also in the third tome of the first book, in which there are thirteen heresies:

**Anaceph. III 34,1a** 34. Marcosians.

**Anaceph. III 34,1b** A certain Mark was a fellow student of Colorbasus, he too introducing two principles.

**Anaceph. III 34,1c** He rejects the resurrection of the dead, and he initiates the women he deceives into certain illusions by means of cups, changing them by incantation into a dark-blue and purple colour.

**Anaceph. III 34,2** And he too will have it that all things are governed by the twenty-four letters, like Valentinus.

**Anaceph. III 35a** 35. Colorbasians.

**Anaceph. III 35b** And this Colorbasus, likewise recounting the same things, but differing in some respect from the other heresies, I mean from the followers of Mark and Valentinus, taught the emanations and ogdoads in a different way.

**Anaceph. III 36,1** 36. The Heracleonites too are carried along by the myth-making about the ogdoads, though otherwise than Mark and Ptolemy and Valentinus and the rest.

**Anaceph. III 36,2** And moreover, at the end, they redeem those of their number who are dying, like Mark, by means of oil, balsam and water, pronouncing certain invocations in Hebrew words over the head of the one supposedly being redeemed.

**Anaceph. III 37** 37. Ophites, who glorify the serpent and hold it to be Christ, and who keep a real snake, this creeping thing, in a kind of basket.

**Anaceph. III 38,1** 38. Cainites likewise, together with the former ones, setting aside both the law and him who spoke in the law, and denying the resurrection of the flesh, glorify Cain, saying that he is of the stronger power.

**Anaceph. III 38,2** And at the same time they also deify Judas, together with the followers of Korah and Dathan and Abiram, and the Sodomites too.

**Anaceph. III 39,1a** 39. Sethians.

**Anaceph. III 39,1b** These in turn glorify Seth, saying that he is from the Mother above, who repented of having emitted Cain and his followers, and then, after Cain had been cast off and Abel killed, came together with the Father above and produced Seth as a pure seed, from whom thereafter the whole race of mankind descended.

**Anaceph. III 39,2** And they too taught principalities and authorities and whatever the others teach.

**Anaceph. III 40,1a** 40. Archontics.

**Anaceph. III 40,1b** These in turn trace the universe back to many archons, and say that the things which have come into being have come into being from them; and they are convicted also of a certain obscenity.

**Anaceph. III 40,2a** The flesh’s resurrection they set aside, and they malign the Old Testament.

**Anaceph. III 40,2b** Yet they also use both the Old and the New Testament, twisting each expression to suit their own mind.

**Anaceph. III 41,1** 41. Cerdonians, the followers of Cerdo, [who] received from Heracleon the succession of his share in the error and made additions to the deceit; having migrated from Syria to Rome, he published his preaching in the times of Bishop Hyginus.

**Anaceph. III 41,2** This man proclaims two principles opposed to each other, and that Christ is not begotten; and in like manner he sets aside both the rising of the dead and the Old Testament.

**Anaceph. III 42,1a** 42. Marcionites.

**Anaceph. III 42,1b** Marcion, whose home was Pontus, was a bishop’s son, but having corrupted a virgin he ran away, because he had been driven out of the church by his own father,

**Anaceph. III 42,2** and having gone up to Rome and asked those of that time [...] for repentance, and not having obtained it, he was lifted up against the faith and laid down doctrines, introducing three principles, good and just and evil, and teaching that the New Testament is alien both to the Old and to him who spoke in it.

**Anaceph. III 42,3a** The flesh’s resurrection he sets aside; he gives not one baptism only, but even two and three after a lapse; and on behalf of catechumens who have died others among them are baptized.

**Anaceph. III 42,3b** And without scruple he permits women too, forsooth, to give the washing.

**Anaceph. III 43a** 43. Lucianists.

**Anaceph. III 43b** A certain Lucian of old, not the one who lived recently in the times of Constantine, laid down doctrines in all things after Marcion, but he too, forsooth, teaches other things beyond Marcion, more extravagantly.

**Anaceph. III 44,1a** 44. Apelleans.

**Anaceph. III 44,1b** This Apelles too, in the same way as Marcion and Lucian, maligns the whole creation and its Maker.

**Anaceph. III 44,2a** But unlike these he did not introduce three principles, but one principle and one God, who is highest and beyond naming, and that this one God made another.

**Anaceph. III 44,2b** And this one who came into being, having been found wicked, made the world in his own baseness.

**Anaceph. III 45,1a** 45. Severians.

**Anaceph. III 45,1b** A certain Severus, again, who followed Apelles, casts off wine and the vine, with the fable that it sprang from the dragon-shaped Satan and the earth when they came together with each other.

**Anaceph. III 45,2** And he refuses woman, declaring her to be of the left-hand power.

**Anaceph. III 45,3a** And he brings in certain names of archons and certain apocryphal books.

**Anaceph. III 45,3b** In the same way as the other heresies, he sets aside the flesh’s resurrection and the Old Testament.

**Anaceph. III 46a** 46. Tatianists.

**Anaceph. III 46b** This man flourished at the same time as Justin, the holy martyr, who was also a philosopher.

**Anaceph. III 46c** But after the death of the martyr and philosopher Justin he was further corrupted by the doctrines of Marcion, having become a disciple of that same man, and he taught the same as he did, adding other things beyond him.

**Anaceph. III 46d** And he was said to come from Mesopotamia.

**Anaceph. III 46e** These are the headings of the first book, in three tomes, of the work against forty-six heresies.

### Sect 34: Marcosians

Against the Marcosians, the fourteenth, but the thirty-fourth in the series.

**34,1,1** A certain Mark, from whom come the so-called Marcosians, starting out from these and daring other things beyond them, spewed out evils upon the world; for he succeeds these, the followers of Secundus and Epiphanes and Ptolemy and Valentinus, but he was lifted up to a larger and more charlatan-like following.

**34,1,2** For he drew after him silly women and men alike, led astray by him, the pitiable fellow being taken to be the improver of the deceivers already mentioned, since he was most expert in magical trickery.

**34,1,3** And having deceived all the men and women mentioned before into giving heed to him as one most gnostic and holding the mightiest power, derived from the invisible and unnameable places, he has been shown to be truly a forerunner of the Antichrist.

**34,1,4** For having mixed the conjuring tricks of Anaxilaus with the knavery of the so-called magi, producing illusions and practising magic by means of them, he threw into amazement those who saw him and believed him, as those who come from him still accomplish this even to this day.

**34,1,5** And those who see the things [...] from curious arts suppose that certain powers are accomplished at his hands and at the hands of those among them who do these things.

**34,1,6a** For having themselves also lost their minds, they do not see, not knowing how to test it, that the contrivance of his trickery is accomplished, so to speak, by magic.

**34,1,6b** For they have become utterly thunderstruck, led on by some evil notion.

**34,1,7** For they say that three cups of clear glass are prepared among them, mixed with white wine, and that during the incantation he performs, the supposed eucharist, they are changed at once, one red [...] like blood, another purple, another dark blue.

**34,1,8a** I, then, lest I hand myself over to a second labour, thought I ought to be content with what was worked out by the most blessed and most holy Irenaeus against this very Mark and those who start out from him, which I have taken pains to set out here word for word; and it is as follows.

**34,1,8b** For Saint Irenaeus himself, in bringing to light what was done by them, speaks thus:

**34,1,8c** From the writings of Saint Irenaeus.

**34,2,1** Pretending to give thanks over cups mixed with wine, and drawing out the word of the invocation at greater length, he makes them appear purple and red, so that it seems that the Grace from those who are above all things is dripping her own blood into his cup through his invocation, and those present yearn beyond measure to have a taste of that drink, in order that the Grace called upon by this magician may shower down on them as well.

**34,2,2a** And again, handing women cups already mixed, he bids them give thanks over them while he stands by.

**34,2,2b** And when this has been done, he himself, bringing forward another cup much larger than the one over which the deluded woman gave thanks, and pouring from the smaller cup, over which the woman gave thanks, into the one brought by him [...], saying at the same time these words, “May she who is before all things,

**34,2,3** the inconceivable and ineffable Grace, fill your inner man and multiply in you her knowledge, sowing the grain of mustard seed in the good earth.”
